# Border control

"Border crossing" redirects here. For other uses, see [Border Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Crossing_\(disambiguation\)).

As seen from the Indian side

The border gate between [Phuentsholing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phuentsholing), Bhutan, and [Jaigaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaigaon), India



The gate that borders [East Nusa Tenggara](453728.md) in [Indonesia](14579.md) and [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor)



A train crossing the [China–Russia border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Russia_border), travelling from [Zabaykalsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zabaykalsk) in Russia to [Manzhouli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manzhouli) in China

Different categories of borders have varying features and levels of security.

**Border control** comprises measures taken by governments to monitor[^cite_note-1] and regulate the movement of people, animals, and goods across land, air, and maritime [borders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border). While border control is typically associated with international borders, it also encompasses controls imposed on [internal borders](#Internal_border_controls) within a single state.

Border control measures serve a variety of purposes, ranging from enforcing [customs](#Customs), sanitary and phytosanitary, or [biosecurity](#Biosecurity) regulations to restricting [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_migration). While some borders (including most states' internal borders and international borders within the [Schengen Area](16565139.md)) are [open](#Open_borders) and completely unguarded, others (including the vast majority of borders between countries as well as some internal borders) are subject to some degree of control and may be crossed legally only at [designated checkpoints](#Border_checkpoints). Border controls in the 21st century are tightly intertwined with intricate systems of travel documents, visas, and increasingly complex policies that vary between countries.

Border controls have high human and economic costs, including tens of thousands of [border deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_death). According to one estimate, the indirect economic cost of border controls is many trillions of dollars, and the size of the global economy could double if migration restrictions were lifted.

## History



The purpose of the [Great Wall of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Wall_of_China) was to stop the "barbarians" from crossing the northern border of China.

In medieval Europe, the boundaries between rival countries and centres of power were largely symbolic or consisted of amorphous borderlands, 'marches', and 'debatable lands' of indeterminate or contested status and the real 'borders' consisted of the fortified walls that surrounded towns and cities, where the authorities could exclude undesirable or incompatible people at the gates, from vagrants, beggars and the 'wandering poor', to 'masterless women', lepers, Romani, or Jews.[^cite_note-2]



Arabic papyrus with an exit permit, dated 24 January 722 AD, pointing to the regulation of travel activities; from Hermopolis Magna, Egypt



Chinese passport from the [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty), 24th Year of the Guangxu Reign, 1898

The concept of border controls has its origins in antiquity. In Asia, the existence of border controls is evidenced in classical texts. The [Arthashastra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthashastra) (c. 3rd century BCE) makes mention of passes issued at the rate of one *[masha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masha_\(unit\))* per pass to enter and exit the country. Chapter 34 of the Second Book of Arthashastra is concerned with the duties of the *_Mudrādhyakṣa_* (lit. 'Superintendent of Seals') who must issue sealed passes before a person could enter or leave the countryside.[^cite_note-3] Passports resembling those issued today were an important part of the Chinese bureaucracy as early as the [Western Han](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Han) (202 BCE-220 CE), if not in the [Qin dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qin_dynasty). They required details such as age, height, and physical features.[^cite_note-\:0-4] The passports (*zhuan*) determined a person's ability to move throughout imperial counties and through points of control. Even children needed passports, but those of one year or less who were in their mother's care may not have needed them.[^cite_note-\:0-4]

### Medieval period

In the Golden age of the [Islamic Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate) (medieval time in Europe), a form of passport was the *bara'a*, a [receipt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Receipt) for taxes paid. Border controls were in place to ensure that only people who paid their *[zakah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat)* (for [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim)) or *[jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya)* (for [dhimmis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi)) taxes could travel freely between different regions of the Caliphate; thus, the *bara'a* receipt was a "basic passport".[^cite_note-5]

In medieval Europe, passports were issued as early as the reign of [Henry V of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_V_of_England) to help his subjects prove who they were in foreign lands. The earliest reference to these documents is found in an act of Parliament, the [Safe Conducts Act 1414](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safe_Conducts_Act_1414) ([2 Hen. 5. Stat. 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Hen._5._Stat._1). c. 6).[^cite_note-brief_history-6][^cite_note-7] In 1540, granting travel documents in England became a role of the [Privy Council of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_England), and it was around this time that the term "passport" was used. In 1794, issuing British passports became the job of the Office of the [Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_\(United_Kingdom\)).[^cite_note-brief_history-6] The 1548 Imperial Diet of Augsburg required the public to hold imperial documents for travel, at the risk of permanent exile.[^cite_note-8] During World War I, European governments introduced border passport requirements for security reasons, and to control the emigration of people with useful skills. These controls remained in place after the war, becoming a standard, though controversial, procedure. British tourists of the 1920s complained, especially about attached photographs and physical descriptions, which they considered led to a "nasty dehumanization".[^cite_note-9]

Beginning in the mid-19th century, the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) established quarantine stations on many of its borders to control disease. For example, along the Greek-Turkish border, all travellers entering and exiting the Ottoman Empire would be quarantined for 9–15 days. Armed guards often manned the stations. If plague appeared, the [Ottoman army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_army) would be deployed to enforce border control and [monitor disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disease_monitoring).[^cite_note-10]

### Modern history



British troops checking the papers of civilians on the [Franco-Belgian border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgium%E2%80%93France_border), 10 October 1939

One of the earliest systematic attempts of modern nation-states to implement border controls to restrict the entry of particular groups was the policy adopted by Canada, Australia, and America to curtail the immigration of Asians in white [settler states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Settler_colonialism) in the late 19th and early 20th centuries. The first anti-East Asian policy implemented in this era was the [Chinese Exclusion Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act) of 1882 in America, which was followed by the [Chinese Immigration Act of 1885](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Immigration_Act_of_1885) in Canada, which imposed what came to be called the [Chinese head tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_head_tax_in_Canada). These policies were a sign of injustice and unfair treatment to the Chinese workers because the jobs they engaged in were mostly menial.[^cite_note-11] Similar policies were adopted in various British colonies in Australia over the latter half of the 19th century targeting Asian immigrants arriving as a result of the region's [series of gold rushes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_gold_rushes)[^cite_note-12] as well as [Kanakas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanakas) (Pacific Islanders brought into Australia as indentured labourers)[^cite_note-13] who alongside the Asians were perceived by [trade unionists](17626.md) and White blue collar workers as a threat to the wages of White settlers.[^cite_note-Markey-14] Following the establishment of the [Commonwealth of Australia](4689264.md) in 1901, these discriminatory border control measures quickly expanded into the [White Australia Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Australia_Policy), while subsequent legislation in America (e.g. the [Immigration Act of 1891](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1891), the [Naturalisation Act of 1906](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalization_Act_of_1906), the [Immigration Act of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1917), and the [Immigration Act of 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1924)) resulted in an even stricter policy targeting immigrants from both Asia and parts of southern and eastern Europe.



British Nationality (Overseas) or BN(O) passports sported a burgundy red cover, identical to that of the [British Citizen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Citizen) passports, albeit without the words "European Union" text at the top part of the cover between 1990 and March 2020.

Even following the adoption of measures such as the [White Australia Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Australia_Policy) and the [Chinese Exclusion Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act) in English-speaking settler colonies, pervasive control of international borders remained a relatively rare phenomenon until the early 20th century, before which many states had open international borders either in practice or due to a lack of any legal restriction. [John Maynard Keynes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes) identified [World War I](4764461.md) in particular as the point when such controls became commonplace.[^cite_note-15]

Decolonisation during the twentieth century saw the emergence of mass emigration from nations in the [Global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_South), thus leading former colonial occupiers to introduce stricter border controls.[^cite_note-newint-16] In the United Kingdom this process took place in stages, with British nationality law eventually shifting from recognising all [Commonwealth citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_citizen) as British subjects to today's complex [British nationality law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_nationality_law) which distinguishes between British citizens, modern [British Subjects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Subjects), [British Overseas Citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Overseas_Citizens), and [overseas nationals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_\(Overseas\)), with each non-standard category created as a result of attempts to balance border control and the need to mitigate [statelessness](789786.md). This aspect of the rise of border control in the 20th century has proven controversial. The [British Nationality Act 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Nationality_Act_1981) has been criticised by experts,[^cite_note-19] as well as by the [Committee on the Elimination of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_on_the_Elimination_of_Racial_Discrimination) of the United Nations,[^cite_note-21] on the grounds that the different classes of British nationality it created are, in fact, closely related to the ethnic origins of their holders.

The creation of British Nationality (Overseas) status, for instance, (with fewer privileges than British citizen status) was met with criticism from many Hong Kong residents who felt that British citizenship would have been more appropriate in light of the "moral debt" owed to them by the UK.[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] Some British politicians[^cite_note-25] and magazines[^cite_note-27] also criticised the creation of BN(O) status. In 2020, the British government under [Boris Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Johnson_ministry) announced a program under which BN(O)s would have leave to remain in the UK with rights to work and study for five years, after which they may apply for [settled status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_leave_to_remain). They would then be eligible for full citizenship after holding settled status for 12 months.[^cite_note-GovUK20200701-28] This was implemented as the eponymously named "British National (Overseas) visa", a residence permit that BN(O)s and their dependent family members have been able to apply for since 31 January 2021.[^cite_note-BNOvisa-29][^cite_note-BNOvisafamily-30] BN(O)s and their dependents who arrived in the UK before the new immigration route became available were granted "Leave Outside the Rules" at the discretion of the [Border Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Force) to remain in the country for up to six months as a temporary measure.[^cite_note-31] In effect, this retroactively granted BN(O)s a path to right of abode in the United Kingdom. Despite the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), about 7,000 people had entered the UK under this scheme between July 2020 and January 2021.[^cite_note-32]

Ethnic tensions created during colonial occupation also resulted in discriminatory policies being adopted in newly independent African nations, such as Uganda under [Idi Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idi_Amin) which banned [Asians from Uganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Asians_from_Uganda), thus creating a mass exodus of the (largely Gujarati[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-toi-34]) Asian community of Uganda. Such ethnically driven border control policies took forms ranging from anti-Asian sentiment in East Africa to [Apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid) policies in South Africa and Namibia (then known as [Southwest Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Africa) under South African rule) which created [bantustans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantustan)[^cite_note-36] and [pass laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pass_laws)[^cite_note-40] to segregate and impose border controls against non-whites, and encouraged immigration of whites at the expense of Blacks as well as Indians and other Asians. Whilst border control in Europe and east of the Pacific have tightened over time,[^cite_note-newint-16] they have largely been liberalized in Africa, from [Yoweri Museveni](293409.md)'s reversal of Idi Amin's anti-Asian border controls[^cite_note-42] to the fall of Apartheid (and thus racialized border controls) in South Africa.

With the development of border control policies over the course of the 20th century came the standardization of [refugee travel documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugee_travel_document) under the [Convention Relating to the Status of Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_Relating_to_the_Status_of_Refugees) of 1951[^cite_note-44] and the [1954 Convention travel document](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Convention_travel_document)[^cite_note-45] for stateless people under the similar 1954 statelessness convention.

### COVID-19

The [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_restrictions_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) in 2020 produced a drastic tightening of border controls across the globe. Many countries and regions have imposed quarantines, entry bans, or other restrictions on citizens of, or recent travellers to, the most affected areas.[^cite_note-46] Other countries and regions have imposed global restrictions that apply to all foreign countries and territories, or prevent their own citizens from travelling overseas.[^cite_note-47] The imposition of border controls has curtailed the spread of the virus, but because they were first implemented after [community spread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_spread) was established in multiple countries in different regions of the world, they produced only a modest reduction in the total number of people infected[^cite_note-48] These strict border controls [economic harm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) to the [tourism industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_industry) through lost income and [social harm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) to people who were unable to travel for family matters or other reasons. When the travel bans are lifted, many people are expected to resume travelling. However, some travel, especially [business travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_travel), may decrease in the long term as lower-cost alternatives, such as [teleconferencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teleconferencing_during_the_2019-20_coronavirus_pandemic) and [virtual events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtual_event), are preferred.[^cite_note-49] A possible long-term impact has been a decline of business travel and international conferencing, and the rise of their virtual, online equivalents.[^cite_note-50] Concerns have been raised over the effectiveness of travel restrictions to contain the spread of [COVID-19](63030231.md).[^cite_note-51]

## Tactics

|  | This article **may lend [undue weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Neutral_point_of_view#Due_and_undue_weight) to details of the "how" and "what" rather than "why", impacts, and alternatives**. Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Border_control#). Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Border_control\&action=edit) by rewriting it in a [balanced fashion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Neutral_point_of_view#Balance) that contextualises different points of view. *(October 2025)*_&#x20;([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Maintenance_template_removal))_ |
| --- | --- |

Contemporary border control policies are complex and address a variety of distinct phenomena depending on the circumstances and political priorities of the state(s) implementing them. Consequently, several aspects of border control vary in nature and importance from region to region.

### Air and maritime borders

In addition to land borders, countries also apply border control measures to [airspace](353557.md) and [waters under their jurisdiction](496983.md). Such measures control access to air and maritime territory, as well as to extractable resources (e.g., [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fishing), minerals, fossil fuels).

Under the [United Nations Convention on the Law of the Sea](31940.md) (UNCLOS),[^cite_note-52] states exercise varying degrees of control over different categories of territorial waters:

- Internal waters: Waters landward of the baseline,

  [^cite_note-baseline-53]

  &#x20;over which the state has complete sovereignty: not even [innocent passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innocent_passage)

  [^cite_note-54]

  &#x20;is allowed without explicit permission from said state. Lakes and rivers are considered internal waters.

- Territorial sea: A state's territorial sea is a belt of [coastal waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_waters) extending at most 22 kilometres from the baseline

  [^cite_note-baseline-53]

  &#x20;of a coastal state. If this overlaps with another state's territorial sea, the border is taken as the median line between the states' baselines, unless the states in question agree otherwise. A state can also choose to claim a smaller territorial sea. The territorial sea is regarded as the sovereign territory of the state. However, foreign ships (military and civilian) are allowed [innocent passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innocent_passage) through it, or [transit passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_passage) for [straits](58646.md); this sovereignty also extends to the [airspace](353557.md#Horizontal_boundary) over and seabed below. As a result of UNCLOS, states exercise a similar degree of control over their territorial sea as over land territory. They may thus utilise coast guard and naval patrols to enforce border control measures, provided they do not prevent innocent or transit passage.

- Contiguous zone: A state's contiguous zone is a band of water extending farther from the outer edge of the territorial sea to up to 44 kilometres (27 miles) from the baseline, within which a state can implement limited border control measures for the purpose of preventing or punishing "infringement of its customs, fiscal, immigration or sanitary laws and regulations within its territory or territorial sea". This will typically be 22 kilometres (14 miles) wide, but could be more (if a state has chosen to claim a territorial sea of less than 22 kilometres), or less, if it would otherwise overlap another state's contiguous zone. However, unlike the territorial sea, there is no standard rule for resolving such conflicts, and the states in question must negotiate their own compromise. America invoked a contiguous zone out to 44 kilometres from the baseline on 29 September 1999.

  [^cite_note-55]

- Exclusive economic zone: An [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) extends from the baseline

  [^cite_note-baseline-53]

  &#x20;to a maximum of 370 kilometres (230 miles). A coastal nation has control of all economic resources within its exclusive economic zone, including fishing, mining, oil exploration, and any pollution of those resources.

  [^cite_note-56]

  &#x20;However, it cannot prohibit passage or loitering above, on, or under the surface of the sea that complies with the laws and regulations adopted by the coastal State in accordance with the provisions of the UN Convention, within that portion of its exclusive economic zone beyond its territorial sea. The only authority a state has over its EEZ is, therefore, its ability to regulate the extraction or spoliation of resources contained therein, and border-control measures implemented to this effect focus on the suppression of unauthorised commercial activity.

Vessels not complying with a state's maritime policies may be subject to [ship arrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ship_arrest) and enforcement action by the state's authorities. Maritime border control measures are controversial in the context of international trade disputes, as was the case following France's detention of British fishermen in October 2021 in the aftermath of Brexit[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58] or when the Indonesian navy detained the crew of the *Seven Seas Conqueress* alleging that the vessel was unlawfully fishing within Indonesian territorial waters while the Singaporean government claimed the vessel was in Singaporean waters near [Pedra Branca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedra_Branca,_Singapore).[^cite_note-59]

Similarly, international law accords each state control over the [airspace](353557.md) above its land territory, internal waters, and territorial sea. Consequently, states have the authority to regulate [flyover rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flyover_rights) and tax foreign aircraft utilising their airspace. Additionally, the [International Civil Aviation Organization](14985.md) designates states to administer international airspace, including airspace over waters that do not form part of any state's territorial sea. Aircraft unlawfully entering a country's airspace may be grounded, and their crews may be detained.

No country has sovereignty over [international waters](394357.md), including the associated airspace. All states have the freedom to fish, navigate, overfly, lay cables and pipelines, and conduct research. Oceans, seas, and waters outside national jurisdiction are also referred to as the high seas or, in [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), *mare liberum* (meaning *free sea*). The 1958 [Convention on the High Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_High_Seas) defined "high seas" to mean "all parts of the sea that are not included in the [territorial sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_sea) or in the [internal waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_waters) of a State" and where "no State may validly purport to subject any part of them to its sovereignty".[^cite_note-60] Ships sailing the high seas are generally under the jurisdiction of their [flag state](6162348.md) (if there is one);[^cite_note-61] however, when a ship is involved in certain criminal acts, such as [piracy](50715.md),[^cite_note-62] any nation can exercise jurisdiction under the doctrine of [universal jurisdiction](48640.md) regardless of maritime borders.

As part of their air and maritime border control policies, most countries restrict or regulate the ability of foreign [airlines](1942.md) and vessels to [transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport) goods or passengers between seaports and airports in their jurisdiction, known as [cabotage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabotage). Restrictions on maritime cabotage exist in most countries with territorial and internal waters to protect the domestic shipping industry from foreign competition, preserve domestically owned shipping infrastructure for national security, and ensure safety in congested territorial waters.[^cite_note-63] For example, in America, the [Jones Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merchant_Marine_Act_of_1920) provides for extremely strict restrictions on cabotage.[^cite_note-64] Similarly, China does not permit foreign flagged vessels to conduct domestic transport or domestic transhipments without the prior approval of the Ministry of Transport.[^cite_note-ukpandi-65] While Hong Kong and Macau maintain distinct internal cabotage regimes from the mainland, maritime cabotage between either territory and the mainland is considered domestic carriage and accordingly is off limits to foreign vessels.[^cite_note-ukpandi-65] Similarly, maritime crossings across the [Taiwan Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan_Strait) require special permits from both the People's Republic of China and the Republic of China[^cite_note-ROC-66] and are usually off-limits to foreign vessels.[^cite_note-ukpandi-65] In India, foreign vessels engaging in the coasting trade[^cite_note-67] require a licence that is generally only issued when no local vessel is available.[^cite_note-ukpandi-65] Similarly, a foreign vessel may only be issued a licence to engage in cabotage in Brazil if there are no Brazilian flagged vessels available for the intended transport.[^cite_note-ukpandi-65]

As with maritime cabotage, most jurisdictions heavily restrict cabotage in passenger aviation, though rules regarding air cargo are typically more lax. Passenger cabotage is not usually granted under most [open skies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_skies) agreements. Air cabotage policies in the [European Union](9317.md) are uniquely liberal insofar as carriers licensed by one member state are permitted to engage in cabotage in any EU member state, with few limitations.[^cite_note-68] [Chile](5489.md) has the most liberal air cabotage rules in the world, enacted in 1979, which allow foreign airlines to operate domestic flights, conditional upon reciprocal treatment for Chilean carriers in the foreign airline's country. Countries apply special provisions to the ability of foreign airlines to carry passengers between two domestic destinations through an offshore hub.[^cite_note-72]

Many countries implement [air defence identification zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_defence_identification_zone) (ADIZs) requiring aircraft approaching within a specified distance of their airspace to contact or seek prior authorization from their military or transport authorities.[^cite_note-Abeyratne-73] An ADIZ may extend beyond a country's territory to give the country more time to respond to possibly hostile aircraft.[^cite_note-WSJNov13-74] The concept of an ADIZ is not defined in any international treaty and is not regulated by any international body,[^cite_note-WSJNov13-74][^cite_note-GlobSec-75] but is nevertheless a well-established aerial border control measure.[^cite_note-78] Usually such zones only cover undisputed territory, do not apply to foreign aircraft not intending to enter territorial airspace, and do not overlap.[^cite_note-GlobSec-75][^cite_note-79]

### Biosecurity



Quarantine operations deployed by mainland Chinese border control



An International Certificate of Vaccination issued by the Bureau of Quarantine in the Philippines to an individual after being vaccinated with a [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md) in 2021

Main articles: [Biosecurity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosecurity), [Quarantine](25237.md), [Travel restrictions related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_restrictions_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic), and [Vaccination requirements for international travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccination_requirements_for_international_travel)

Biosecurity refers to measures aimed at preventing the introduction and/or spread of harmful [organisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organism) (e.g., [viruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virus), [bacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacteria), etc.) to [animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal) and [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant) to mitigate the risk of transmission of [infectious disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infectious_disease). In [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture), these measures are aimed at protecting [food crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_crop) and [livestock](25160767.md) from [pests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pest_\(organism\)), [invasive species](67554.md), and other organisms not conducive to the welfare of the [human population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_population). The term includes [biological threats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_pest_control) to people, such as [pandemic](24255.md) diseases and [bioterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioterrorism). The definition has sometimes been broadened to embrace other concepts, and it is used for different purposes in different contexts. The most common category of biosecurity policies is quarantine measures adopted to counteract the spread of disease and, when applied as part of border control, primarily focus on mitigating the entry of infected individuals, plants, or animals into a country.[^cite_note-80] Other aspects of biosecurity related to border control include mandatory vaccination policies for inbound travellers and measures to curtail the risk posed by [bioterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioterrorism) or [invasive species](67554.md). Quarantine measures are frequently implemented regarding the mobility of animals, including both pets and livestock. Notably, to reduce the risk of introducing [rabies](16946852.md) from continental Europe, the United Kingdom used to require that dogs and most other animals introduced into the country spend six months in quarantine at an [HM Customs and Excise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Her_Majesty's_Customs_and_Excise) pound. This policy was abolished in 2000 in favour of a scheme generally known as [Pet Passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pet_Passports), under which animals can avoid quarantine if they have documentation showing they are up to date with their appropriate [vaccinations](32653.md).[^cite_note-81]

In the past, European countries implemented quarantine measures to curb the [Bubonic Plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubonic_Plague) and [Cholera](7591.md). In the British Isles, for example, the [Quarantine Act 1710](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarantine_Act_1710) ([9 Ann.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9_Ann.) c. 2) established maritime quarantine policies in an era in which strict border control measures as a whole were yet to become mainstream.[^cite_note-82] The first act was called for due to fears that the plague might be imported from Poland and the [Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states). A second act of Parliament, the [Quarantine Act 1721](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarantine_Act_1721) ([8 Geo. 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8_Geo._1). c. 10), was due to the prevalence of the plague at [Marseille](40888948.md) and other places in [Provence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provence,_France), France. It was renewed in 1733 after a new outbreak in [continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe), and again in 1743, due to an epidemic in [Messina](59705.md). A rigorous quarantine clause was introduced into the Levant Act 1752, an act regulating trade with [the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Levant), and various arbitrary orders were issued during the next twenty years to meet the supposed danger of infection from the Baltic states. Although no plague cases ever came to England during that period, the restrictions on traffic became more stringent, and a very strict Quarantine and Customs Act 1788 ([28 Geo. 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/28_Geo._3). c. 34) was passed, with provisions affecting cargoes in particular. The act was revised in 1801 and 1805, and in 1823–24 an elaborate inquiry was followed by an act making quarantine only at the discretion of the [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom), which recognised yellow fever or other highly infectious diseases as calling for quarantine, along with plague. The threat of cholera in 1831 was the last occasion in England when quarantine restrictions were used. Cholera affected every country in Europe despite all efforts to keep it out. When cholera returned to England in 1849, 1853, and 1865–66, no attempt was made to seal the ports. In 1847 the privy council ordered all arrivals with a clean [bill of health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_of_health) from the [Black Sea](3386.md) and the Levant to be admitted, provided there had been no case of plague during the voyage and afterwards, the practice of quarantine was discontinued.[^cite_note-Booker-83]

In modern [maritime law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_law), biosecurity measures for arriving vessels centre on 'pratique', a licence issued by border control officials permitting a ship to enter port, on the assurance of the captain that the vessel is free from [contagious disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contagious_disease). The clearance granted is commonly referred to as 'free pratique'. A ship can signal a request for 'pratique' by flying a solid yellow square-shaped flag. This yellow flag is the [**Q** flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_flag_\(contagion\)) in the set of [international maritime signal flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_maritime_signal_flags).[^cite_note-84] In the event that 'free pratique' is not granted, a vessel will be held in quarantine according to biosecurity rules prevailing at the port of entry until a border control officer inspects the vessel.[^cite_note-85] During the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), a controversy arose as to who granted pratique to the *[Ruby Princess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruby_Princess)*.[^cite_note-86] A related concept is the 'bill of health', a document issued by officials of a [port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port) of departure indicating to the officials of the port of arrival whether it is likely that the ship is carrying a [contagious disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contagious_disease), either literally on board as [fomites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fomite) or via its crewmen or passengers. As defined in a consul's handbook from 1879:

> A bill of health is a document issued by the consul or the public authorities of the port which a ship sails from, descriptive of the health of the port at the time of the vessel's clearance. A *clean* bill of health certifies that at the date of its issue no infectious disease was known to exist either in the port or its neighbourhood. A *suspected* or *touched* bill of health reports that rumours were in circulation that an infectious disease had appeared but that the rumour had not been confirmed by any known cases. A *foul* bill of health or the absence of a clean bill of health implies that the place the vessel cleared from was infected with a contagious disease. The two latter cases would render the vessel liable to quarantine.[^cite_note-87]

Another category of biosecurity measures adopted by border control organisations is [mandatory vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccination_requirements_for_international_travel). As a result of the prevalence of [Yellow Fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Fever) across much of the African continent, a significant portion of countries in the region require arriving passengers to present an [International Certificate of Vaccination or Prophylaxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Certificate_of_Vaccination_or_Prophylaxis) (Carte Jaune) certifying that they have received the [Yellow Fever vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_Fever_vaccine). A variety of other countries require travelers who have visited areas where Yellow Fever is endemic to present a certificate to clear border checkpoints, as a means of preventing the spread of the disease. Before the emergence of [COVID-19](63030231.md), Yellow Fever was the primary human disease subject to [de facto](43594.md) [vaccine passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunity_passport) measures by border control authorities worldwide. Similar measures are in place regarding [Polio](25107.md) and [meningococcal meningitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meningococcal_meningitis) in regions where those diseases are endemic and in countries bordering those regions. Before the [eradication of smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox#Eradication), similar Carte Jaune requirements were in force for that disease around the world.



A road sign at an exit on [Interstate 91](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_91) in [Vermont](32578.md) advising individuals entering the state of a quarantine policy adopted in response to COVID-19, photographed in November 2020



[Slovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Slovakia), which otherwise maintains open borders with other [Schengen Area](16565139.md) jurisdictions, closed borders to non-residents because of the COVID-19 pandemic.

As a result of the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), biosecurity measures have become a highly visible aspect of border control across the globe. Most notably, quarantine and [mandatory COVID-19 vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccination_requirements_for_international_travel#COVID-19) for international travelers. Together with a decreased willingness to travel, the implementation of biosecurity measures has had a [negative economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) and [social impact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) on the [travel industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_industry).[^cite_note-88] Slow travel gained popularity during the pandemic, with tourists visiting fewer destinations on their trips.[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90]

Biosecurity measures such as restrictions on cross-border travel, the introduction of mandatory vaccination for international travellers, and the adoption of quarantine or mandatory testing measures have helped to contain the spread of COVID-19.[^cite_note-\:0b-91] While test-based border screening measures may prove effective under certain circumstances, they may fail to detect a significant quantity of positive cases if only conducted upon arrival without follow-up. A minimum 10-day quarantine may help prevent the spread of COVID-19 and may be more effective when combined with additional control measures, such as border screening.[^cite_note-\:0b-91] A study in *[Science](193513.md)* found that travel restrictions could delay the initial arrival of COVID-19 in a country, but that they produced only modest overall effects unless combined with domestic [infection prevention and control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection_prevention_and_control) measures to reduce transmissions considerably.[^cite_note-92] (That is consistent with prior research on influenza and other communicable diseases.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-\:2-94]) Travel bans early in the pandemic were most effective for isolated locations, such as small [island nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_nation).[^cite_note-\:2-94]

During the COVID-19 pandemic, many jurisdictions around the world introduced biosecurity measures at internal borders. This ranged from quarantine measures imposed upon individuals crossing state lines within America to [prohibitions on interstate travel in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia).

### Customs

Main article: [Customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs)



North Korean customs officer at [Pyongyang Sunan International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyongyang_Sunan_International_Airport)



Southern edge (customs border) of Captain Cook wharf, [Ports of Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ports_of_Auckland), New Zealand; an [electric fence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_fence) is faintly visible behind the historical fence

Each country has its own laws and regulations for the [import](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_trade) and [export](307592.md) of goods into and out of a country, which its customs authority enforces. The import or export of certain goods may be restricted or prohibited, in which case customs controls enforce these policies.[^cite_note-Chowdhury_1992-95] Customs enforcement at borders can also entail collecting [excise tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excise_tax) and preventing the smuggling of dangerous or illegal goods. A [customs duty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_duty) is a [tariff](55551.md) or [tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax) on the importation (usually) or exportation (unusually) of goods.

In many countries, border controls for arriving passengers at many international airports and some road crossings are separated into red and green channels to prioritise customs enforcement.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] Within the European Union's common customs area, airports may operate additional blue channels for passengers arriving from within that area. For such passengers, border control may focus specifically on prohibited items and other goods that are not covered by the common policy. [Luggage tags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bag_tag) for checked luggage travelling within the EU are green-edged to facilitate identification.[^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99] In most EU member states, travellers coming from other EU countries within the [Schengen Area](16565139.md) can use the green lane. However, airports outside the Schengen Area, or those with frequent flights arriving from jurisdictions within the Schengen Area but outside the European Union, may use blue channels for convenience and efficiency.

A customs area is a designated area for storing commercial goods that have not cleared border controls. Commercial goods not yet cleared through customs are often stored in a customs area known as a [bonded warehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonded_warehouse) until processed or re-exported.[^cite_note-ANTW-100][^cite_note-101] Ports authorized to handle international cargo generally include recognised bonded warehouses. For [customs duties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_duties), goods within the customs area are treated as being outside the country. This allows easy [transshipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transshipment) to a third country without the involvement of customs authorities.[^cite_note-ANTW-100] For this reason, customs areas are usually carefully controlled and fenced to prevent [smuggling](28645.md). However, the area is still part of the country's territory. Hence, the goods within the area are subject to other local laws (for example, [drug laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_laws) and [biosecurity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosecurity) regulations) and may thus be searched, [impounded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Search_and_seizure), or turned back. The term is also sometimes used to define an area (usually composed of several countries) that forms a [customs union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_union), a [customs territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_territory), or to describe the area at airports and ports where travellers are checked through customs.

Sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) measures are customs measures to protect humans, animals, and plants from diseases, pests, or contaminants. The [Agreement on the Application of Sanitary and Phytosanitary Measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreement_on_the_Application_of_Sanitary_and_Phytosanitary_Measures), concluded at the [Uruguay Round of the Multilateral Trade Negotiations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruguay_Round_of_the_Multilateral_Trade_Negotiations), establishes the types of SPS measures that each jurisdiction may impose. Examples of SPS are tolerance limits for residues, restricted use of substances, labelling requirements related to food safety, hygienic requirements, and quarantine requirements. In certain countries, sanitary and phytosanitary measures focus extensively on curtailing and regulating the import of foreign agricultural products to protect domestic ecosystems. For example, Australian border controls restrict most (if not all) food products, certain wooden products, and other similar items.[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] Similar restrictions exist in Canada, America and New Zealand.



Map depicting countries with a policy of executing drug traffickers \
&#x20;

&#x20; Only under certain conditions

&#x20; [Capital punishment for drug offences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_for_drug_trafficking)

Border control in many countries in Asia and the Americas prioritizes enforcing customs laws pertaining to narcotics. For instance, India and Malaysia are focusing resources on eliminating drug smuggling from Myanmar and Thailand, respectively. The issue stems largely from the high output of dangerous and illegal drugs in the [Golden Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Triangle_\(Southeast_Asia\)) as well as in regions further west, such as Afghanistan. A similar problem exists east of the Pacific. It has resulted in countries such as Mexico and the United States tightening border control in response to the northward flow of illegal substances from regions such as Colombia. The [Mexican drug war](8442323.md) and similar cartel activity in neighboring areas have exacerbated the problem. In certain countries, illegal importing, exporting, selling, or possessing drugs constitutes capital offences and may result in a death sentence. A 2015 article by *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* says that the laws of 32 countries provide for capital punishment for drug smuggling. Still, only in six countries – [China](5405.md), [Iran](14653.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md), [Vietnam](202354.md), [Malaysia](3607937.md), and [Singapore](27318.md) –are drug offenders known to be routinely executed.[^cite_note-105] Additionally, Singapore, Malaysia, and Indonesia impose mandatory death sentences on individuals caught smuggling restricted substances across their borders. For example, Muhammad Ridzuan Ali was executed in Singapore on 19 May 2017, for drug trafficking.[^cite_note-106] According to a 2011 article by the [Lawyers Collective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawyers_Collective), an [NGO](46539.md) in [India](14533.md), "32 countries impose [capital punishment](5902.md) for offences involving [narcotic drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcotic_drug) and [psychotropic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychotropic) substances."[^cite_note-107] South Korean law provides for capital punishment for drug offences,[^cite_note-108] but [South Korea](27019.md) has a de facto moratorium on capital punishment as there have been no executions since 1997, even though there are still people on death row and new death sentences continue to be handed down.[^cite_note-109]

### Border security

"Border security" redirects here. For other uses, see [Border Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security_\(disambiguation\)).

Border security refers to measures taken by one or more governments to enforce their border control policies.[^cite_note-110] Such measures target a variety of issues, ranging from customs violations and trade in unlawful goods to the suppression of unauthorized [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_migration) or travel. The specific border security measures taken by a jurisdiction vary with local authorities' priorities and are influenced by social, economic, and geographical factors. Some countries use [deadly force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deadly_force) to [deter](735661.md) [illegal entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_entry).[^cite_note-a336-111] Sanctions for attempted illegal entry were found to deter repeated attempts ([recidivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recidivism)).[^cite_note-y788-112]

In India, which maintains free movement with Nepal and Bhutan, border security focuses primarily on the Bangladeshi, Pakistani, and Myanmar borders. India's primary focus with regard to the [border with Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93India_border) is to deter unlawful immigration and drug trafficking.[^cite_note-channel4-113] On the Pakistani border, the [Border Security Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security_Force) aims to prevent the infiltration of Indian territory by terrorists from Pakistan and other countries in the west (Afghanistan, Iraq, Syria, etc.). In contrast, India's border with Myanmar is porous and the [2021 military coup](66567997.md) in Myanmar saw an influx of refugees seeking asylum in border states including [Mizoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizoram).[^cite_note-114] The [refoulement](2702190.md) of [Rohingya](1918632.md) refugees is a contentious aspect of India's border control policy vis à vis Myanmar.[^cite_note-115]

Meanwhile, American border security policy is largely centred on the country's [border with Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border). Security along this border comprises many distinct elements, including physical barriers, patrol routes, lighting, and border patrol personnel. In contrast, the [border with Canada](630260.md) is primarily maintained through joint border patrol and security camera programs, making it the world's longest undefended border. In remote areas along the border with Canada, where staffed border crossings are unavailable, there are hidden [sensors](235757.md) on roads, trails, railways, and wooded areas near crossing points.[^cite_note-116]

Members of the Schengen Agreement are required to apply strict checks on travellers entering and exiting the area. These checks are coordinated by the European Union's [Frontex](3072700.md) agency, and subject to common rules. The details of border controls, surveillance and the conditions under which permission to enter into the Schengen Area may be granted are exhaustively detailed in the Schengen Borders Code.[^cite_note-schengen_borders_code-117] All persons crossing external borders are subject to a check by a [border guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_guard). The only exception is for regular cross-border commuters (both those with the [right of free movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directive_2004/38/EC_on_the_right_to_move_and_reside_freely) and third-country nationals) who are well known to the border guards: once an initial check has shown that there is no alert on record relating to them in the [Schengen Information System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_Information_System) or national databases, they can only be subject to occasional 'random' checks, rather than systematic checks every time they cross the border.[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] Additionally, border security in Europe is increasingly being outsourced to private companies, with the border security market growing at a rate of 7% per year.[^cite_note-121] In its Border Wars series, the Transnational Institute showed that the arms and security industry helps shape European border security policy through lobbying, regular interactions with the EU's border institutions, and its shaping of research policy.[^cite_note-122] The institute criticises the border security industry for having a vested interest in increasing border militarisation to increase profits. Furthermore, the same companies are also often involved in the arms trade and thus profit twice: first from fuelling the conflicts, repression, and human rights abuses that have led refugees to flee their homes and later from intercepting them along their migration routes.[^cite_note-123]

#### Border walls

Border walls are a common aspect of border security measures worldwide. Border walls generally seek to limit unauthorised travel across an international border and are frequently implemented as a [populist](8354382.md) response to refugees and economic migrants.

The [India-Bangladesh barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh%E2%80%93India_border#India%E2%80%93Bangladesh_barrier) is a 3,406 kilometres (2,116 miles) long fence of barbed wire and concrete just under 3 metres (9 feet 10 inches) high, currently under construction.[^cite_note-Centre_has_fenced_3406_km_along_Indo-Bangladesh_border-124] Its stated aim is to limit unauthorised migration.[^cite_note-channel4-113] The project has run into several delays; there is no clear completion date for the project yet.[^cite_note-Centre_has_fenced_3406_km_along_Indo-Bangladesh_border-124] Similar to India's barrier with Bangladesh and the proposed wall between America and Mexico, Iran has constructed a [wall on its frontier with Pakistan](11715031.md). The wall aims to reduce unauthorised border crossings[^cite_note-125] and stem the flow of drugs,[^cite_note-126] and is also a response to terrorist attacks, notably the one in the Iranian border town of [Zahedan](1044089.md) on 17 February 2007, which killed thirteen people, including nine [Iranian Revolutionary Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Revolutionary_Guard) officials.[^cite_note-127] President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s proposal to build [a new wall along the border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_barrier) formed a major feature of his 2016 presidential campaign and over the course of his presidency, his administration spent approximately US\$15 billion on the project, with US\$5 billion appropriated from [US Customs and Border Protection](757040.md), US\$6.3 billion appropriated from anti-narcotics initiative funded by congress and US\$3.6 billion appropriated from the American military.[^cite_note-128] Members of both the [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_the_United_States) & [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_of_the_United_States) parties who opposed Trump's border control policies regarded the border wall as unnecessary or undesirable, arguing that other measures would be more effective at reducing illegal immigration than building a wall, including tackling the economic issues that lead to immigration being a relevant issue altogether, border surveillance or an increase in the number of customs agents.[^cite_note-129] Additionally, in August 2020, the United States constructed 3.8 km of short cable fencing along the border between [Abbotsford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbotsford,_British_Columbia), British Columbia, and [Whatcom County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whatcom_County,_Washington), Washington.[^cite_note-130]

Border walls have formed a major component of European border control policy following the [European migrant crisis](46415102.md). The [walls at Melilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melilla_border_fence) and [at Ceuta](859281.md) on Spain's border with Morocco are designed to curtail refugees' and migrants' ability to enter the European Union via the two Spanish cities on the Moroccan coast. Similar measures have been taken on the Schengen area's borders with Turkey in response to the refugee crisis in Syria. The creation of the European Union's collective border security organisation, [Frontex](3072700.md), is another aspect of the bloc's growing focus on border security. Within the Schengen Area, border security has become an especially prominent priority for the Hungarian government under right-wing [strongman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strongman_\(politics\))[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] Viktor Orbán.[^cite_note-141] Similarly, [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) has begun construction of a border barrier or fence between its territory and [Yemen](350939.md) to prevent the unauthorized movement of people and goods. The disparity in the countries' economic conditions means that many Yemenis head to Saudi Arabia to find work. Saudi Arabia does not have a barrier with its other neighbours in the [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md), whose economies are more similar to its own. In 2006 Saudi Arabia proposed constructing a security fence along the entire length of its 900 kilometre long desert border with Iraq.[^cite_note-142] As of July 2009 it was reported that Saudis will pay \$3.5 billion for a security fence.[^cite_note-infoprod-143] The combined wall and ditch will be 965 kilometres (600 miles) long and include five layers of fencing, watch towers, [night-vision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night-vision) cameras, and radar cameras and manned by 30,000 troops.[^cite_note-144] Elsewhere in Europe, the [Republic of Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Macedonia) began erecting a fence on its border with Greece in November 2015.[^cite_note-145]

In 2003, [Botswana](3464.md) began building a 480 kilometres (300 miles) long [electric fence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_fence) along its border with [Zimbabwe](34399.md). The official reason for the fence is to stop the spread of [foot-and-mouth disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foot-and-mouth_disease) among livestock. Zimbabweans argue that the fence's height is clearly intended to keep people out. Botswana has responded that the fence is designed to keep out cattle and to ensure that entrants have their shoes disinfected at legal border crossings. Botswana also argued that the government continues to encourage legal movement into the country. Zimbabwe was unconvinced, and the barrier remains a source of tension.[^cite_note-146]

#### Border checkpoints

Main articles: [Border checkpoint](288329.md), [Port of entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_entry), and [International airport](1519705.md)

Further information: [International zone § Sterile zones at ports of entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_zone#Sterile_zones_at_ports_of_entry)

Sultan Iskandar Building, Johor Bahru, Malaysia

[Sultan Iskandar Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Iskandar_Building)

Woodlands Checkpoint, Singapore

[Woodlands Checkpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor%E2%80%93Singapore_Causeway#Woodlands_Checkpoint)

The Sultan Iskandar Building ([Malaysia](3607937.md)) and Woodlands Checkpoint ([Singapore](27318.md)) on the [Malaysia–Singapore border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia%E2%80%93Singapore_border) handles the busiest international land border crossing in the world, with 350,000 travellers daily prior to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).

[^cite_note-147]

[^cite_note-\:1-148]



The International Terminal at [San Francisco International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_International_Airport) is an airport of entry for travellers entering America and has border checkpoint facilities for arriving passengers. Like most North American airports, it does not have a [sterile international transit area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_zone#Sterile_zones_at_ports_of_entry). Domestic and international passengers are thus not clearly separated. That enables easier transfers from domestic to international flights but requires international transit passengers to clear the border checkpoint even if their final destination is outside America.

A Border checkpoint is a place where goods or individuals moving across borders are inspected for compliance with border control measures. Access-controlled borders often have a limited number of checkpoints where they can be crossed without legal sanctions. Arrangements or treaties may be established to allow or mandate less-restrained crossings (e.g., the [Schengen Agreement](179592.md)). Land border checkpoints (land ports of entry) can be contrasted with the [customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs) and [immigration](4599312.md) facilities at [seaports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port), [international airports](1519705.md), and other [ports of entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_entry).

Checkpoints generally serve two purposes:

- To prevent the entrance of individuals who are either undesirable (e.g., criminals or others who pose threats) or simply unauthorised to enter.

- To prevent the entrance of goods or contaminants that are illegal or subject to restriction, or to collect [tariffs](55551.md) in accordance with [customs](#Customs) or [quarantine](#Quarantine) policies.

A border checkpoint at which travellers are permitted to enter a jurisdiction is known as a port of entry. [International airports](1519705.md) are usually ports of entry, as are road and rail crossings on a land [border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border). [Seaports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port) can be used as ports of entry only if a dedicated [customs](#Customs) presence is posted there. The decision to become a port of entry is up to the civil authority controlling the port. An airport of entry is an [airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport) that provides customs and immigration services for incoming flights. These services allow the airport to serve as an initial port of entry for foreign visitors arriving in the country. While the terms airport of entry and international airport are generally used interchangeably, not all international airports qualify as airports of entry, since some international airports lack immigration or customs facilities in the Schengen Area, where member states have eliminated border controls with one another. Airports of entry are usually larger than [domestic airports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_airport) and often feature longer [runways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Runway) and facilities to accommodate the heavier aircraft commonly used for international and intercontinental travel. International airports often also serve [domestic flights](30208174.md), which help feed both passengers and cargo into international flights (and vice versa). Buildings, operations, and management have become increasingly sophisticated since the mid-20th century, when international airports began to provide infrastructure for international civilian flights. Detailed technical standards have been developed to ensure safety, and common [coding systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airline_codes) have been implemented to provide global consistency. The physical structures that serve millions of individual passengers and flights are among the most complex and interconnected in the world. By the second decade of the 21st century, there were over 1,200 international airports and almost two billion international passengers, along with 50 million metric tons (49,000,000 long tons; 55,000,000 short tons) of cargo passing through them annually.

Border inspections are also meant to protect each country's [agriculture from pests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_pest).[^cite_note-Already-Present-149] National and international [phytosanitary authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phytosanitary_authorities) maintain [databases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Database) of border interceptions, occurrences, and [establishments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Established_species).[^cite_note-Already-Present-149] Bebber *et al.*, 2019 analyzes such records and finds that they underreport many important pest species, that [island nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_nation) are more vulnerable because they have lower border-to-area ratios, and that pests are moving [poleward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geographical_pole) to follow humans' [crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop) as our crops follow [global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming).[^cite_note-Already-Present-149]

A 'Quilantan' or 'Wave Through' entry is a phenomenon at American border checkpoints authorising a form of non-standard but legal entry without any inspection of travel documents. It occurs when border security personnel at a border crossing admit some individuals without conducting a standard interview or document review.[^cite_note-150] If an individual can prove that they were waved through immigration in this manner, then they are considered to have entered with inspection despite not having answered any questions or received a passport [entry stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passport_stamp).[^cite_note-151] This definition of legal entry only extends to foreigners who entered America at official border crossings and does not provide a path to legal residency for those who did not enter through a recognised crossing.[^cite_note-152]

- Sterile lounge at Changi Airport Terminal 3 in Singapore. Passengers transiting between international flights at Terminals 1, 2, and 3 of Changi Airport disembark directly into the sterile lounge, and may proceed to their connecting gate without clearing the border checkpoint or completing any other border control formality, and can move between terminals using the Skytrain without clearing any checkpoint.

  Sterile lounge at [Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changi_Airport) Terminal 3 in [Singapore](27318.md). Passengers transiting between international flights at Terminals 1, 2, and 3 of Changi Airport disembark directly into the [sterile lounge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_zone#Sterile_zones_at_ports_of_entry), and may proceed to their connecting gate without clearing the border checkpoint or completing any other border control formality, and can move between terminals using the [Skytrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changi_Airport_Skytrain) without clearing any checkpoint.

While the HK-Macau Ferry Terminal is a port of entry for travellers from other Chinese cities in the Pearl River Delta, the Ocean Terminal is a port of entry for visitors arriving by cruise ship from a wider range of jurisdictions.

While the HK-Macau Ferry Terminal is a port of entry for travellers from other Chinese cities in the Pearl River Delta, the Ocean Terminal is a port of entry for visitors arriving by cruise ship from a wider range of jurisdictions.

#### Border zones

"Border zone" redirects here. For the video game, see [*Border Zone* (video game)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Zone_\(video_game\)).

See also: [International zone § Demilitarised zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_zone#Demilitarised_zones)

Border zones are areas near borders that have special restrictions on movement. Governments may forbid unauthorised entry to or exit from border zones and restrict property ownership in the area. The zones function as [buffer zones](489870.md), specifically monitored by [border patrols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_guard), to prevent unauthorised cross-border travel. Border zones enable authorities to detain and prosecute individuals suspected of being or aiding undocumented migrants, smugglers, or spies without necessarily having to prove that the individuals in question actually engaged in the suspected unauthorised activity, since, as all unauthorised presence in the area is forbidden, the mere presence of an individual permits authorities to arrest them. Border zones between hostile states can be heavily militarised, with [minefields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minefield), barbed wire, and [watchtowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watchtower). Some border zones are designed to prevent illegal immigration or emigration and have few restrictions, but may operate checkpoints to verify immigration status. In most places, a [border vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_vista) is usually included and/or required. In some nations, movement inside a border zone without a licence is an offence and will result in arrest. No probable cause is required as mere presence inside the zone is an offence, if it is intentional.[^cite_note-153] Even with a license to enter, photography, making fires, and carrying of firearms and hunting may be prohibited.

Examples of international border zones are the [Border Security Zone of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_Security_Zone_of_Russia) and the Finnish border zone on the [Finnish–Russian border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish%E2%80%93Russian_border). There are also intra-country zones such as the [Cactus Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cactus_Curtain) surrounding the [Guantanamo Bay Naval Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_Naval_Base) in Cuba, the [Korean Demilitarised Zone](328874.md) along the [North Korea](21255.md)-[South Korea](27019.md) demarcation line and the [Frontier Closed Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontier_Closed_Area) in [Hong Kong](13404.md). Important historical examples are the [Wire of Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wire_of_Death) set up by [the German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_German_Empire) to control the [Belgium–Netherlands border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgium%E2%80%93Netherlands_border) and the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain), a set of border zones maintained by the Soviet Union and its satellite states along their borders with Western states. One of the most militarised parts was the [restricted zone of the inner German border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_German_border#Restricted_zone). While the zone was initially and officially for border security, it was eventually engineered to prevent escape from the Soviet sphere into the West. Ultimately, the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) governments resorted to using lethal countermeasures against those trying to cross the border, such as mined fences and orders to shoot anyone trying to cross into the West. The restrictions on building and habitation made the area a "green corridor", which today is known as the [European Green Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Belt).

In the area stretching inwards from its [internal border](#Internal_border_controls) with the [mainland](55079.md), Hong Kong maintains a [Frontier Closed Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frontier_Closed_Area) [out of bounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusion_zone) to those without special authorisation. The area was established in the 1950s, when Hong Kong was under British administration, as a result of the [Convention for the Extension of Hong Kong Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_for_the_Extension_of_Hong_Kong_Territory) before the [transfer of sovereignty over Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transfer_of_sovereignty_over_Hong_Kong) in 1997. The area's purpose was to prevent illegal immigration and smuggling; smuggling had become prevalent as a consequence of the [Korean War](16772.md). Today, under the [one country, two systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_country,_two_systems) policy, the area continues to be used to curtail unauthorized migration to Hong Kong and the smuggling of goods in either direction.



South Korean policemen standing guard at North Korea-South Korea border; view from South Korea



North Korean policemen standing guard at North Korea-South Korea border; view from North Korea

As a result of the partition of the Korean peninsula by America and the Soviet Union after World War II, and exacerbated by the subsequent [Korean War](16772.md), there is a [Demilitarised Zone](328874.md) (DMZ) spanning the *[de facto](43594.md)* border between [North](21255.md) and [South Korea](27019.md). The DMZ follows the effective boundaries as of the end of the Korean War in 1953. Similar to the Frontier Closed Area in Hong Kong, this zone and the defence apparatus on both sides of the border serve to curtail unauthorised passage between them. In South Korea, there is an additional fenced-off area between the Civilian Control Line (CCL) and the start of the Demilitarized Zone. The CCL is a line that designates an additional buffer zone to the Demilitarized Zone, located 5 to 20 kilometres (3.1 to 12.4 miles) from the Southern Limit Line of the Demilitarized Zone. Its purpose is to limit and control civilian entry into the area to protect and maintain the security of military facilities and operations near the Demilitarized Zone. The commander of the [8th US Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_United_States_Army) ordered the creation of the CCL, and it was activated and first became effective in February 1954.[^cite_note-154] The buffer zone that falls south of the Southern Limit Line is called the Civilian Control Zone. Barbed wire fences and manned military guard posts mark the CCL. South Korean soldiers typically accompany tourist buses and cars travelling north of the CCL as armed guards to monitor the civilians as well as to protect them from North Korean intruders. Most tourist and media photos of the "Demilitarised Zone fence" are actually of the CCL fence. The actual Demilitarized Zone fence along the Southern Limit Line is completely off-limits to everyone except soldiers, and it is illegal to photograph it.

Similarly, the whole estuary of the [Han River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_River_\(Korea\)) in the Korean Peninsula is deemed a "Neutral Zone" and is officially off-limits to all civilian vessels. Only [military vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_vessel) are allowed within this neutral zone.[^cite_note-156] In recent years, Chinese fishing vessels have taken advantage of the tense situation in the [Han River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_River_\(Korea\)) Estuary Neutral Zone and illegally fished in this area due to both [North Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_People's_Navy) and [South Korean](2284275.md) navies never patrolling this area due to the fear of naval battles breaking out. This has led to [firefights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naval_combat) and sinkings of boats between Chinese fishermen and [South Korean Coast Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Korean_Coast_Guard).[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] On 30 January 2019, North Korean and South Korean military officials signed a landmark agreement that would open the Han River Estuary to civilian vessels for the first time since the Armistice Agreement in 1953. The agreement was scheduled to take place in April 2019, but the failure of the [2019 Hanoi Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Hanoi_Summit) indefinitely postponed these plans.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161]

The [Green Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Buffer_Zone_in_Cyprus) separating [Southern Cyprus](5593.md) and [Northern Cyprus](271409.md) is a demilitarised border zone operated by the [United Nations Peacekeeping Force in Cyprus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Peacekeeping_Force_in_Cyprus)[^cite_note-162] operate and patrol within the buffer zone. The buffer zone was established in 1974 due to ethnic tensions between [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Cypriots) and [Turkish Cypriots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Cypriots). The green line is similar in nature to the [38th parallel](328874.md) separating the Republic of Korea and North Korea.

&#x20;Some border zones, referred to as **border vistas**, are composed of legally mandated cleared space between two areas of foliage located at an international border intended to provide a clear demarcation line between two jurisdictions. Border vistas are most commonly found along undefended international boundary lines, where border security is not as much of a necessity and a built barrier is undesired, and are a treaty requirement for certain borders. An example of a border vista is a 6-metre (20-foot) cleared space around unguarded portions of the [Canada–United States border](630260.md).[^cite_note-163] Similar clearings along the border line are provided for by many international treaties. For example, the 2006 border management treaty [between Russia and China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Russia_border) provides for a 15-metre (49-foot) cleared strip along the two nations' border.[^cite_note-164]

In 2024, Egypt announced that they are building a buffer zone on the Egypt-Gaza border.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166]
