Immigration law refers to the national [statutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute), [regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_and_secondary_legislation), and [legal precedents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precedent) governing [immigration](4599312.md) into and [deportation](412990.md) from a country. Strictly speaking, it is distinct from other matters such as [naturalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturalisation) and [citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship), although they are often conflated. Immigration laws vary around the world and according to the social and [political climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_climate) of the times, as acceptance of immigrants shifts from the widely inclusive to the deeply [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalism) and [isolationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolationism). Countries frequently maintain laws that regulate both entry and exit rights and internal rights, such as the duration of stay, [freedom of movement](1270497.md), and the right to participate in commerce or government. National laws regarding the immigration of citizens of that country are regulated by [international law](8195726.md). The [United Nations'](31769.md) [International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Civil_and_Political_Rights) mandates that all countries allow entry to their own citizens.[^cite_note-167]

### Immigration policies

#### Diaspora communities



*Karta Polaka* – specimen document

Certain countries adopt immigration policies designed to be favourable towards members of diaspora communities with a connection to the country. For example, the Indian government confers [Overseas Citizenship of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Citizenship_of_India) (OCI) status on foreign citizens of [Indian origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-resident_Indian_and_person_of_Indian_origin) to enable them to live and work indefinitely in India. OCI status was introduced in response to demands for [dual citizenship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_citizenship) by the Indian diaspora, particularly in countries with large populations of Indian origin.[^cite_note-168] It was introduced by *The Citizenship (Amendment) Act, 2005* in August 2005.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170] Similar to OCI status, the [UK Ancestry visa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_Ancestry_visa) exempts members of the British diaspora[^cite_note-172] from usual immigration controls. Poland issued the [Karta Polaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karta_Polaka) to citizens of certain northeast European countries with Polish ancestry, but later expanded it to the worldwide [Polonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_diaspora).

Some nations recognise a [right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_return) for people with ancestry in that country or a connection to a particular ethnic group. A notable example of this is the right of [Sephardi Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardi_Jews) to acquire Spanish nationality by virtue of their community's Spanish origins. Similar exemptions to immigration controls exist for people of Armenian origin seeking to acquire Armenian citizenship. Ghana similarly grants an indefinite right to stay in Ghana to members of the [African diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_diaspora), regardless of citizenship.[^cite_note-173] Similarly, Israel maintains a policy permitting members of the [Jewish diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_diaspora) to [immigrate to Israel](351513.md) regardless of prior nationality.

South Korean immigration policy is relatively unique in that, as a consequence of its claim over the territory currently administered by North Korea, citizens of North Korea are regarded by the South as its own citizens by birth.[^cite_note-174] As a result, North Korean refugees in China often attempt to travel to countries such as Thailand which, while not offering asylum to North Koreans, classifies them as unauthorized immigrants and deports them to South Korea instead of North Korea.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177] At the same time, the policy has operated to prevent pro-North Korea [Zainichi Koreans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zainichi_Koreans) recognised by Japan as [Chōsen-seki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C5%8Dsen-seki) from entering South Korea without special permission from the South Korean authorities as, despite being regarded as citizens of the Republic of Korea and members of the Korean diaspora, they generally refuse to exercise that status.[^cite_note-178]

#### Open borders

Main article: [Open border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_border)



&#x20; Member states of the Schengen area

&#x20; Other member states of the European Union

An open border is the [deregulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation) and/or lack of regulation of the movement of persons between nations and jurisdictions; this does not apply to trade or movement between privately owned land areas.[^cite_note-179] Most nations have open borders for travel within their nation of travel, though more authoritarian states may limit the freedom of internal movement of its citizens, as [for example](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propiska_in_the_Soviet_Union) in the former USSR. However, only a handful of nations have deregulated open borders with other nations, an example of this being European countries under the [Schengen Agreement](179592.md) or the open [Belarus-Russia border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belarus-Russia_border).[^cite_note-180] Open borders used to be very common among all nations, however this became less common after the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War), which led to the regulation of open borders, making them less common and no longer feasible for most [industrialised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation) nations.[^cite_note-181]

Open borders are the norm for borders between subdivisions within the boundaries of [sovereign states](23604120.md). However, some countries do maintain [internal border controls](#Internal_border_controls) (for example between the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) mainland and the [special administrative regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_administrative_regions_of_China) of [Hong Kong](13404.md) and [Macau](19068.md); or between the American mainland, the unincorporated territories[^cite_note-182] other than Puerto Rico, and Hawaii.[^cite_note-184] Open borders are also usual between member states of [federations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation), however, movement between member states is controlled in exceptional circumstances.[^cite_note-185] Federations, confederations, and similar multinational unions typically maintain external border controls through a collective border control system. However, they sometimes have open borders with other non-member states through special international agreements –  such as between Schengen Agreement countries as mentioned above.

Presently, open border agreements of various types are in force in several areas around the world, as outlined below:

- *Asia and Oceania*:&#x20;

  - Under the [1950 Indo-Nepal Treaty of Peace and Friendship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_Indo-Nepal_Treaty_of_Peace_and_Friendship), India and Nepal maintain an arrangement similar to the CTA and the Union State. Indians and Nepalis are not subject to any migration controls in each other's countries, and there are few controls on land travel by citizens across the border.

  - India and Bhutan also have a similar programme in place. The border between Jaigaon, in the Indian state of [West Bengal](34040.md), and the city of Phuentsholing is essentially open, and although there are internal checkpoints, Indians (as outlined under the [Visa policy of Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Bhutan) are allowed to proceed throughout Bhutan with a [voter's ID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_ID_\(India\)) or an identity slip from the Indian consulate in Phuentsholing. Similarly, Bhutanese passport holders enjoy free movement in India.

  - Thailand and Cambodia: Whilst not as liberal as the policies concerning the Indo-Nepalese and Indo-Bhutanese borders, Thailand and Cambodia have begun issuing combined visas to certain categories of tourists applying at specific Thai or Cambodian embassies and consulates, enabling freer border crossings between the two countries.

    [^cite_note-nationmultimedia-186]

    &#x20;The policy is currently in force for nationals of America and several European (primarily EU, EEA, and GCC) and Oceanian countries as well as for Indian and Chinese nationals residing in Singapore.

    [^cite_note-187]

  - Australia and New Zealand: Similar to the agreement between India and Nepal, the [Trans-Tasman Travel Arrangement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Tasman_Travel_Arrangement) between Australia and New Zealand is a free movement agreement citizens of each country to travel freely between them and allowing citizens and some permanent residents to reside, visit, work, study in the other country for an indefinite period, with some restrictions.

    [^cite_note-188]

    [^cite_note-189]

    &#x20;The arrangement came into effect in 1973, and allows citizens of each country to reside and work in the other country, with some restrictions. Other details of the arrangement have varied over time. From 1 July 1981, all people entering Australia (including New Zealand citizens) have been required to carry a passport. Since 1 September 1994, Australia has had a universal visa requirement, and to specifically cater for the continued free movement of New Zealanders to Australia, the [Special Category Visa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Category_Visa) was introduced for New Zealanders.

- *Central America* : The [Central America-4 Border Control Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_America-4_Border_Control_Agreement) abolishes border controls for land travel between El Salvador, Honduras, Nicaragua, and Guatemala. However, this does not apply to air travel.

- *Europe and the Middle East*&#x20;

  - Union State of Russia and Belarus The [Union State of Russia and Belarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_State_of_Russia_and_Belarus) is a supranational union of Russia and Belarus, which eliminates all border controls between the two nations. Before a visa agreement was signed in 2020, each country maintained its own visa policies, resulting in non-citizens of both countries generally being barred from travelling directly between them. However, since the visa agreement was signed, each side recognises the other's visas, meaning third-country citizens can enter both countries with a visa from either country.

    [^cite_note-190]

  - Western Europe: The two most significant free travel areas in Western Europe are the [Schengen Area](16565139.md), in which very little, if any, border control is generally visible, and the [Common Travel Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Travel_Area) (CTA), which partially eliminates such controls for nationals of the United Kingdom and Ireland. Between countries in the Schengen Area and, to an extent, within the CTA on the British Isles, internal border control is often virtually unnoticeable. It is typically carried out only through random searches of cars or trains in the [hinterland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinterland). In contrast, controls at borders with non-member states may be rather strict.

  - Gulf Cooperation Council: Members of the [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md), or GCC, allow each other's citizens freedom of movement in an arrangement similar to the CTA and to that between India and Nepal. Between 5 June 2017 and 5 January 2021, freedom of movement in Saudi Arabia, the UAE, and Bahrain was suspended for Qataris as a result of the [Saudi-led blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar_diplomatic_crisis) of the country.

#### Hostile environment policies

Further information: [Home Office hostile environment policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Office_hostile_environment_policy), [National Register of Citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Citizens), [National Register of Citizens for Assam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Citizens_for_Assam), and [Immigration reduction in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_reduction_in_the_United_States)

Certain jurisdictions gear their immigration policies toward creating a hostile environment for undocumented migrants to deter migration by creating an unwelcoming atmosphere for potential and existing immigrants. Notably, the British [Home Office](225695.md) adopted a set of administrative and legislative measures designed to make staying in the [United Kingdom](31717.md) as difficult as possible for people without [leave to remain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indefinite_leave_to_remain), in the hope that they may "[voluntarily leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-deportation)".[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] The Home Office policy was first announced in 2012 under the [Conservative-Liberal Democrat coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameron%E2%80%93Clegg_coalition).[^cite_note-196] The policy was implemented pursuant to the 2010 [Conservative Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\)) Election Manifesto.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199] The policy has been criticized for being unclear, has led to many incorrect threats of deportation and has been called "Byzantine" by the [England and Wales Court of Appeal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Appeal_\(England_and_Wales\)) for its complexity.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206]

Similarly, [anti-immigration movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_reduction_in_the_United_States) in America have advocated for policies aimed at creating a hostile environment for intended and existing immigrants at various points in history. Historical examples include the [nativist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nativism_\(politics\)) [Know Nothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Know_Nothing) movement of the mid-19th century, which advocated hostile policies against [Catholic](606848.md) immigrants; the [Workingman's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workingman's_Party), which promoted xenophobic attitudes toward Asians in [California](5407.md) during the late-19th century, a sentiment that ultimately led to the [Chinese Exclusion Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Exclusion_Act) of 1882; the [Immigration Restriction League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Restriction_League), which advocated xenophobic policies against southern and eastern Europe during the late-19th and early 20th centuries, and the joint congressional [Dillingham Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dillingham_Commission). After [World War I](4764461.md), these cumulatively resulted in the highly restrictive [Emergency Quota Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Quota_Act) of 1921 and the [Immigration Act of 1924](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_Act_of_1924). Over the first two decades of the 21st century, the [Republican Party](32070.md) adopted an increasingly nativist platform, advocating against [sanctuary cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_city) and in favour of building a [wall with Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_barrier) and reducing the number of immigrants permitted to settle in the country. Ultimately, the Trump administration furthered many of these policy goals, including the adoption of harsh policies such as the [Remain in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remain_in_Mexico) and [family separation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_administration_family_separation_policy) policies vis-à-vis refugees and migrants arriving from Central America via Mexico. [Islamophobic](162173.md) policies such as the [travel ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_travel_ban) targeted primarily at Muslim-majority countries also feature prominently in attempts to create a hostile environment for immigrants perceived by [populists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_wing_populism) as not belonging to the predominant [WASP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Anglo-Saxon_Protestants) culture in the United States.

India's [citizenship registration policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Citizens) serves to create a hostile environment for the country's Muslim community in the regions in which it has been implemented.[^cite_note-207] The Indian government is presently in the process of building several detention camps throughout India in order to detain people not listed on the register.[^cite_note-208] On 9 January 2019, the Union government released a '2019 Model Detention Manual', which said that every city or district, having a major immigration check post, must have a detention centre.[^cite_note-209] The guidelines suggest detention centres with 3 metres (9 feet 10 inches) high boundary walls covered with barbed wires.[^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211]

### International zones

Main article: [International zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_zone)

An international zone is any area [not fully subject](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraterritoriality) to the border control policies of the state in which it is located. There are several types of international zones, ranging from special economic zones and sterile zones at ports of entry exempt from customs rules to concessions over which administration is ceded to one or more foreign states. International zones may also maintain distinct visa policies from the rest of the surrounding state.

## Effects

Further information: [Economic effects of immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_effects_of_immigration)

The direct cost of border control is hundreds of billions to the United States government alone.[^cite_note-212] Although the indirect economic costs of migration restrictions are much less well studied than those of [trade barriers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_barriers),[^cite_note-213] some economists estimate that the indirect economic cost of border controls cost many trillions of dollars and the size of the global economy could double if migration restrictions were lifted.[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-215] Immigration can in some cases reduce wages[^cite_note-g315-216] or worsen economic [life-cycle assessments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life-cycle_assessment).[^cite_note-i933-217]

Global border controls have been associated with more than 63,000 [border deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_death) since 2000,[^cite_note-Kiriscioglu-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-Dehm-220] although scholars estimate that border deaths are significantly underestimated.[^cite_note-Soto-221][^cite_note-Obinna-222][^cite_note-223] Governments often blame unauthorized border crossers for taking excessive risks.[^cite_note-Steinhilper-224][^cite_note-Cuttitta-225] Some unauthorized border crossers describe they choose dangerous methods of border crossing when safe and legal avenues are closed.[^cite_note-Kiriscioglu-218][^cite_note-Dehm-220][^cite_note-Steinhilper-224][^cite_note-226] In some cases, enforcement tactics have rerouted migrants to more dangerous routes with the stated goal to deter border crossers with the increased danger.[^cite_note-Obinna-222][^cite_note-Soto-221][^cite_note-Dehm-220] Efforts to make unauthorized immigration more difficult may promote permanent settlement in place of earlier patterns of temporary migration.[^cite_note-227]

## Internal border controls

Main article: [Internal border control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_border_control)

Further information: [Travel document § Internal travel documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_document#Internal_travel_documents)

|  | This section **may be [too long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Article_size) to read and navigate comfortably**. Consider [splitting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) content into sub-articles, [condensing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Summary_style) it, or adding [subheadings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Section#Subsections). Please discuss this issue on the article's [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Border_control). *(April 2022)* |
| --- | --- |

Internal border controls are measures implemented to control the flow of people or goods within a given country. Such measures take a variety of forms, ranging from the imposition of border checkpoints to the issuance of internal travel documents, and vary depending on the circumstances in which they are implemented. Circumstances resulting in internal border controls include increasing security around border areas (e.g., internal checkpoints in America or Bhutan near border regions), preserving the autonomy of autonomous or minority areas (e.g., border controls between Peninsular Malaysia, Sabah, and Sarawak; border controls between Hong Kong, Macau, and mainland China), preventing unrest between ethnic groups (e.g., Northern Ireland's peace walls, border controls in Tibet and Northeastern India), and disputes between rival governments (e.g., between the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China) and the [People's Republic of China](5405.md)).

During the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), temporary internal border controls were introduced in jurisdictions across the globe. For instance, travel between Australian states and territories was prohibited or restricted by state governments at various points of the pandemic, either in conjunction with sporadic lockdowns or as a stand-alone response to COVID-19 outbreaks in neighbouring states.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] Internal border controls were also introduced at various stages of Malaysia's [Movement Control Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_movement_control_order), per which interstate travel was restricted depending on the severity of ongoing outbreaks. Similarly, internal controls were introduced by national authorities within the Schengen Area, though the [European Union](9317.md) ultimately rejected the idea of suspending the [Schengen Agreement](179592.md) per se.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232]

### Asia

Internal border controls exist in many parts of Asia. For example, travellers visiting minority regions in India and China often require special permits to enter.[^cite_note-234] Internal air and rail travel within non-autonomous portions of India and mainland China also generally require travel documents to be checked by government officials as a form of interior border checkpoint. For such travel within India, Indian citizens may utilise their [Voter ID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_ID_\(India\)), [National Identity Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aadhaar), passport, or other proof of Indian citizenship, whilst Nepali nationals may present any similar proof of Nepali citizenship. For such travel within mainland China, Chinese nationals must use their national identity cards.

#### China

Within China, extensive border controls are maintained for those travelling between the [mainland](55079.md) and the [special administrative regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Administrative_Region_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) of [Hong Kong](13404.md) and [Macau](19068.md). Foreign nationals need to present their passports or other required travel documents when travelling between these jurisdictions. For Chinese nationals (including those with [British National (Overseas)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_National_\(Overseas\)) status), there are special [documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_document)[^cite_note-235] for travel between these territories. Internal border controls in China have also led to the creation of special permits that allow Chinese citizens to immigrate to or reside in other immigration areas within the country.[^cite_note-236]

China also maintains distinct, relaxed border control policies in the [Special Economic Zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Economic_Zone) of [Shenzhen](83368.md), [Zhuhai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhuhai) and [Xiamen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiamen).[^cite_note-voa-237][^cite_note-238] Nationals of most countries[^cite_note-240] can obtain a limited area visa upon arrival in these regions, which permits them to stay within these cities without proceeding further into other parts of mainland China. Visas for Shenzhen are valid for 5 days, and those for Xiamen and Zhuhai are valid for 3 days. The duration of stay starts the day after arrival.[^cite_note-241] The visa can only be obtained only upon arrival at [Luohu Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luohu), [Huanggang Port Control Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huanggang_Port_Control_Point), [Fuyong Ferry Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuyong_Ferry_Terminal) or [Shekou Passenger Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shekou_Passenger_Terminal) for Shenzhen;[^cite_note-242] [Gongbei Port of Entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gongbei_Port_of_Entry), [Hengqin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hengqin) Port or [Jiuzhou Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiuzhou_Port) for Zhuhai;[^cite_note-243] and [Xiamen Gaoqi International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiamen_Gaoqi_International_Airport) for Xiamen.[^cite_note-244]

Similarly, China permits nationals of non—visa-exempt [ASEAN](106539.md) countries[^cite_note-245] to visit [Guilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilin) without a visa for a maximum of 6 days if they travel with an approved tour group and enter China from [Guilin Liangjiang International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilin_Liangjiang_International_Airport). They may not visit other cities within [Guangxi](47836950.md) or other parts of mainland China.[^cite_note-246]

Neither the People's Republic of China nor the Republic of China recognises the passports issued by the other, and neither considers travel between mainland China and [areas controlled by the Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_area_of_the_Republic_of_China)[^cite_note-ROC-66] as formal international travel. There are arrangements in place for travel between territories controlled by the [Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_China) and territories controlled by the [People's Republic of China](5405.md).[^cite_note-247]

More generally, authorities in [mainland China](55079.md) maintain a system of residency registration known as *[hukou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hukou_system)* ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language): 户口; lit. 'household individual'), by which government permission is needed to change one's place of residence formally. It is enforced using [identity cards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resident_Identity_Card). This system of internal border control measures effectively limited internal migration before the 1980s, but subsequent market reforms caused it to collapse as a means of migration control. An estimated 150 to 200 million people are part of the "blind flow" and have unofficially migrated, generally from poor rural areas to wealthy urban areas. However, unofficial residents are often denied official services such as education and medical care and are sometimes subject to both social and political [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination). In essence, the denial of social services outside an individual's registered area of residence serves as an internal border control measure aimed at dissuading migration within the mainland.

#### Bhutan

Meanwhile, in Bhutan, accessible by road only through India, there are interior border checkpoints (primarily on the [Lateral Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateral_Road)) and, additionally, certain areas require [special permits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Bhutan#Restricted_areas_permit) to enter, whilst visitors not proceeding beyond the border city of [Phuentsholing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phuentsholing) do not need permits to enter for the day (although such visitors are [de facto](43594.md) subject to Indian visa policy since they must proceed through [Jaigaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaigaon)). Individuals who are not citizens of India, Bangladesh, or the Maldives must obtain both their visa and any regional permits required through a licensed tour operator before arriving in the country. Citizens of India, Bangladesh, and the Maldives may apply online for regional permits to restricted areas.[^cite_note-248]

#### Malaysia

Another example is the [Malaysian](3607937.md) states of [Sabah](28678.md) and [Sarawak](28258.md), which have maintained their own border controls[^cite_note-249] since joining Malaysia in 1963. The internal border control is asymmetrical: while Sabah and Sarawak impose immigration controls on Malaysian citizens from other states, there is no corresponding border control in [Peninsular Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsular_Malaysia), and Malaysians from Sabah and Sarawak have an unrestricted right to live and work in the Peninsular. For social and business visits of less than three months, Malaysian citizens may travel between the Peninsular, Sabah, and Sarawak using the [Malaysian identity card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_identity_card) (MyKad) or [Malaysian passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysian_passport), while for longer stays in Sabah and Sarawak, they are required to have an Internal Travel Document or a passport with the appropriate residential permit.

#### North Korea

The most restrictive internal border controls are in [North Korea](21255.md). Citizens are not allowed to travel outside their areas of residence without explicit authorisation, and access to the capital city, Pyongyang, is heavily restricted.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] Similar restrictions are imposed on tourists, who are only allowed to leave Pyongyang on government-authorised tours to approved tourist sites.

### Europe

An example from Europe is the implementation of border controls on travel to and from [Svalbard](27441.md), which maintains a policy of [free migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_migration) under the [Svalbard Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Svalbard_Treaty) and the Schengen Area, which includes the rest of Norway. Other examples of effective internal border controls in Europe include the [closed cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closed_cities) of certain [CIS](36870.md) members, [areas of Turkmenistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Turkmenistan#Special_permits) that require special permits to enter, [restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Tajikistan#Gorno-Badakhshan) on travel to the [Gorno-Badakhshan Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorno-Badakhshan_Autonomous_Region) in Tajikistan, and (depending on whether Northern and Southern Cyprus are considered separate countries) the Cypriot border. Similarly, Iraq's [Kurdistan region](679693.md) maintains a separate, more liberal visa and customs regime from the rest of the country, even allowing [visa-free entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Iraq) for Israelis, whilst the rest of the country bans them from entering. Denmark also maintains a complex system of subnational countries which, unlike the Danish mainland, are outside the European Union and maintain autonomous customs policies.[^cite_note-252] In addition to the numerous closed cities of Russia,[^cite_note-253] parts of 19 subjects[^cite_note-254] of the Russian Federation are closed for foreigners without special permits and are consequently subject to internal border controls.[^cite_note-255]

Another complex border-control situation in Europe involves the United Kingdom. Whilst the [crown dependencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_dependencies) are within the [Common Travel Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Travel_Area), neither [Gibraltar](7607314.md) nor the sovereign British military exclaves of [Akrotiri and Dhekelia](1025144.md) are. The former maintains its own [border control policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policies_of_British_Overseas_Territories#Gibraltar), thus requiring physical border security at its border with the Schengen Area, as well as implementing border controls for travellers travelling directly between Gibraltar and the British mainland. The latter maintains a relatively open border with Southern Cyprus, though not with Northern Cyprus. Consequently, it is a de facto member of the Schengen Area, and travel to or from the British mainland is subject to border controls. On 31 December 2020, Spain and the United Kingdom reached an agreement in principle under which Gibraltar would join the Schengen Area,[^cite_note-256] clearing the way for the European Union and the UK to start formal negotiations on the matter.[^cite_note-257]

In the aftermath of [Brexit](41688778.md), border controls for goods flowing between Great Britain and Northern Ireland were introduced in accordance with the [Protocol on Ireland/Northern Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Protocol) agreed to as part of the UK's [withdrawal agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_withdrawal_agreement) with the EU.[^cite_note-nipa-258] Due to the [thirty-year internecine conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Troubles) in Northern Ireland, the [UK-Ireland border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Ireland%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_border) has had a special status since that conflict was ended by the [Belfast Agreement/Good Friday Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday_Agreement) of 1998. As part of the [Northern Ireland Peace Process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_Peace_Process), the border has been largely invisible, without any physical barrier or custom checks on its many crossing points; this arrangement was made possible by both countries' common membership of both the [EU's Single Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Single_Market) and [Customs Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Customs_Union) and of their [Common Travel Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Travel_Area). Upon the UK's withdrawal from the European Union, the border in Ireland became the only land border between the UK and the EU. EU single market and [UK internal market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_Internal_Market_Act_2020) provisions require certain customs checks and trade controls at their external borders. The Northern Ireland Protocol is intended to protect the EU single market, while avoiding imposition of a '[hard border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit_and_the_Irish_border#Hard_border)' that might incite a recurrence of conflict and destabilize the relative peace that has held since the end of [the Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Troubles). Under the Protocol, Northern Ireland is formally outside the EU single market, but [EU free movement of goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Single_Market) rules and [EU Customs Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_Customs_Union) rules still apply; this ensures there are no customs checks or controls between Northern Ireland and the rest of the island. In place of an Ireland/Northern Ireland land border, the protocol has created a *[de facto](43594.md)* customs [border down the Irish Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Sea_border) for customs purposes, separating Northern Ireland from the island of Great Britain,[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260] to the disquiet of prominent [Unionists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster_Unionism).[^cite_note-BBC_56737351-261] To operate the terms of the protocol, the United Kingdom must provide border control posts at Northern Ireland's ports:[^cite_note-BBC_56737351-261] actual provision of these facilities is the responsibility of Northern Ireland's [Department of Agriculture, Environment and Rural Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Agriculture,_Environment_and_Rural_Affairs) (DAERA).[^cite_note-BBC_56737351-261] Temporary buildings were put in place for 1 January 2021, but in February 2021, the responsible Northern Ireland minister, [Gordon Lyons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Lyons) (DUP), ordered officials to stop work on new permanent facilities and to stop recruiting staff for them.[^cite_note-262] In its half yearly financial report on 26 August 2021, [Irish Continental Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Continental_Group), which operates ferries between Great Britain and the Republic of Ireland, expressed concern at the lack of implementation of checks on goods arriving into Northern Ireland from Great Britain, as required under the protocol. The company said that the continued absence of these checks (on goods destined for the Republic of Ireland) is causing a distortion of the level playing field, since goods that arrive directly at the Republic of Ireland's ports from Great Britain are checked on arrival.[^cite_note-263] The implementation of border controls between Great Britain and Northern Ireland was the primary catalyst for the [2021 Northern Ireland riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Northern_Ireland_riots).

An unusual example of internal border controls pertains to customs enforcement within the Schengen area. Even though borders are generally invisible, the existence of areas within the Schengen area but outside the [European Union Value Added Tax Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Value_Added_Tax_Area), as well as jurisdictions such as Andorra which are not officially a part of the Schengen area but can not be accessed without passing through it, has resulted in the existence of sporadic [internal border controls](16565139.md#Internal_controls) for customs purposes. Additionally, as per Schengen area rules,[^cite_note-264] hotels and other types of commercial accommodation must register all foreign citizens, including citizens of other Schengen states, by requiring the completion of a registration form by their own hand.[^cite_note-266] The Schengen rules do not require any other procedures; thus, the Schengen states are free to regulate further details on the content of the registration forms, and identity documents which are to be produced, and may also require the persons exempted from registration by Schengen laws to be registered. A Schengen state is also permitted to reinstate border controls with another Schengen country for a short period where there is a serious threat to that state's "public policy or internal security" or when the "control of an external border is no longer ensured due to exceptional circumstances".[^cite_note-267] When such risks arise out of foreseeable events, the state in question must notify the European Commission in advance and consult with other Schengen states.[^cite_note-268] Since the implementation of the Schengen Agreement, this provision has been invoked frequently by member states, especially in response to the [European migrant crisis](46415102.md).[^cite_note-271]

### Middle East

The [Israeli military](39237.md) maintains an intricate network of internal border controls within the [Israeli-occupied](2781576.md) [West Bank](33209.md) as well as external border controls between the West Bank and Israel, restricting the [freedom of movement of Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_freedom_of_movement), composed of [permanent, temporary, and random manned checkpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_checkpoint) in the West Bank; the [West Bank Barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Bank_Barrier); and restrictions on the usage of roads by Palestinians.[^cite_note-272] Spread throughout the areas of the [State of Palestine](241405.md) under *[de facto](43594.md)* Israeli control, internal border control measures are a key feature of Palestinian life and are among the most restrictive in the world. Additionally, the [blockade](19224310.md) of the [Gaza Strip](12047.md) results in a de facto domestic customs and immigration border for Palestinians. To clear internal border controls, Palestinians are required to [obtain a variety of permits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_permit_regime_in_the_West_Bank) from Israeli authorities, depending on the purpose and area of their travel. The legality and impact of this network of internal border controls are controversial. [B'Tselem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B'Tselem), an Israeli [non-governmental organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-governmental_organisation) that monitors [human rights](13831.md) in Palestine, argues that they breach the rights guaranteed by the [International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Economic,_Social_and_Cultural_Rights)—in particular, the right to a livelihood, the right to an acceptable standard of living, the right to satisfactory nutrition, clothing, and housing, and the right to attain the best standard of physical and mental health.[^cite_note-273] B'Tselem also argues that the restrictions on ill, wounded and pregnant Palestinians seeking acute medical care is in contravention of international law that states that medical professionals and the sick must be granted open passage.[^cite_note-274] While [Israeli Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Supreme_Court) has deemed the measures acceptable for security reasons, *Haaretz*'s [Amira Hass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amira_Hass) argues this policy defies one of the principles of the Oslo Accords, which states that Gaza and the West Bank constitute a single geographic unit.[^cite_note-275]

Strained intercommunal relations in Northern Ireland between [Irish Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_Catholics) and [the descendants of Protestant settlers from England and Scotland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster_Protestants) have resulted in *de facto* internal checkpoints. The [peace lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_lines) are an internal border security measure to separate predominantly [republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_republicanism) and [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irish_nationalism) Catholic neighbourhoods from predominantly [loyalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulster_loyalism) and [unionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unionism_in_Ireland) Protestant neighbourhoods. They have been in place in some form or another since the end of [The Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Troubles) in 1998, with the [Good Friday Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Friday_Agreement). The majority of peace walls are located in [Belfast](5046.md), but they also exist in [Derry](9055.md), [Portadown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portadown), and [Lurgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lurgan),[^cite_note-NIF-276] with more than 32 kilometres (20 miles) of walls in Northern Ireland.[^cite_note-277] The peace lines range in length from a few hundred metres to over 5 kilometres. They may be made of iron, brick, steel, or a combination of the three and are up to 8 metres (26 feet) high.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-NIF-276] Some have gates in them (sometimes staffed by [police](294068.md)) that allow passage during daylight but are closed at night.

### North America

See also: [Hyder-Stewart Border Crossing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyder-Stewart_Border_Crossing), [Cornwall Island (Ontario)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall_Island_\(Ontario\)), and [Akwesasne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akwesasne)



American Samoa entry stamp



Hyder, Alaska, has no border controls for travellers entering from Canada, and travellers flying between Hyder and other Alaskan cities by seaplane undergo internal border control.

Multiple types of internal border controls exist in the United States. While the American territories of [Guam](11974.md) and the [Northern Mariana Islands](21402.md) follow the [same visa policy as the mainland](4766461.md), together, they also maintain their own visa waiver programme for certain nationalities.[^cite_note-279] Since the two territories are outside the [customs territory of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_territory_of_the_United_States), there are customs inspections when travelling between them. The rest of the U.S. [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa) has its own customs and immigration regulations, thus travelling between it and other American jurisdictions involves both customs and immigration inspections. The [Virgin Islands](32135.md) are a special case: they fall within the American immigration zone and follow American visa policy, but are a customs-free territory. As a result, there are no immigration checks between the two, but travellers arriving in Puerto Rico or the American mainland directly from the Virgin Islands are subject to border control for customs inspection. The United States also maintains [interior checkpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Border_Patrol_interior_checkpoints), similar to those maintained by Bhutan, along its borders with Mexico and Canada, subjecting people to border controls even after they have entered the country.

The [Akwesasne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akwesasne) nation; with territory in [Ontario](22218.md), [Quebec](7954867.md), and [New York](8210131.md); features several de facto internal border controls. As a result of protests by Akwesasne residents on their rights to cross the border unimpeded, as provided under the 1795 [Jay Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Treaty), the [Canada Border Services Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_Border_Services_Agency) closed its post on [Cornwall Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall_Island_\(Ontario\)), instead requiring travellers to proceed to the checkpoint in the city of [Cornwall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornwall,_Ontario). As a consequence of the arrangement, residents of the island are required to clear border controls when proceeding North to the Ontario mainland, as well as when proceeding South to Akwesasne territory in New York, thereby constituting internal controls from both a Canadian and an Akwesasne perspective. Similarly, travelling between Canada and the Quebec portion of the Akwesasne nation requires driving through the state of New York, meaning individuals must clear American controls when leaving Quebec proper and Canadian border controls when entering Quebec proper. However, Canada does not impose border controls when entering the Quebec portion of the Akwesasne Nation. Nevertheless, for residents who assert a [Haudenosaunee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haudenosaunee) national identity distinct from Canadian or American citizenship, the intricate network of Canadian and American border controls is seen as a foreign-imposed system of internal border controls, similar to the Israeli checkpoints in Palestinian territory.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281]

The city of [Hyder, Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyder,_Alaska), has also been subject to internal border controls since America chose to stop regulating arrivals in Hyder from British Columbia. Since travellers exiting Hyder into Stewart, British Columbia, are subject to Canadian border controls, it is theoretically possible for someone to accidentally enter Hyder from Canada without their travel documents and then face difficulties, as both the U.S. and Canada would subject them to border controls that require travel documents. At the same time, however, the northern road connecting Hyder to the uninhabited mountain regions of British Columbia has neither American nor Canadian border controls, meaning that tourists from Canada proceeding northwards from Hyder are required to complete Canadian immigration formalities when they return to Stewart, despite never having cleared American immigration.

### Historical

Identification and freedom of internal movement have sometimes been instruments of oppression, for example, in Canada's [pass system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pass_system_\(Canadian_history\)), or [Apartheid-era South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid-era_South_Africa)'s [Pass laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pass_laws).

## Specific requirements



American and Canadian border officers at the [Vancouver](32706.md) airport

The degree of strictness of border controls varies across countries and borders. In some countries, controls may be targeted at the traveller's religion, ethnicity, nationality, or countries visited. Others may need to ensure the traveller has paid the appropriate fees for their [visas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_\(document\)) and has future travel planned outside the country. Yet others may concentrate on the contents of the traveller's baggage and imported goods to ensure nothing is being carried that might pose a [biosecurity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosecurity) risk to the country.

### Biometrics

See also: [Biometric identification by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biometric_identification_by_country)



A fingerprint scanner at [Dulles International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dulles_International_Airport) collects biometric data on visitors, which can be used for confirming identities.

Several countries[^cite_note-295] require all travellers, or all foreign travelers, to be [fingerprinted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fingerprint) on arrival and refuse admission to or arrest travellers who refuse to comply. In some countries, such as America, this may apply even to transit passengers proceeding to a third country.[^cite_note-296] Many countries also require a photo to be taken of people entering the country. The United States, which does not fully implement [exit control](#Exit_controls) formalities at its land frontiers (although long mandated by domestic legislation),[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] intends to implement [facial recognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facial_recognition_system) for passengers departing from international airports to identify people who overstay their visa.[^cite_note-300] Together with fingerprint and face recognition, [iris scanning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_recognition) is one of three biometric identification technologies internationally standardised since 2006 by the [International Civil Aviation Organization](14985.md) (ICAO) for use in [e-passports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-passport)[^cite_note-301] and the United Arab Emirates conducts iris scanning on visitors who need to apply for a visa.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] The [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) has announced plans to increase the biometric data it collects at American borders greatly.[^cite_note-304] In 2018, Singapore began trials of iris scanning at three land and maritime immigration checkpoints.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306]

### Immigration stamps

Main article: [Passport stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passport_stamp)

An immigration stamp is an inked impression in a [passport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passport) or other travel document, typically made by a rubber stamp upon entering or exiting a territory. Depending on the jurisdiction, a stamp can serve different purposes. For example, in the United Kingdom, an immigration stamp in a passport includes the formal [leave to enter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leave_to_enter) granted to a person subject to entry control. In other countries, a stamp activates or acknowledges the continuing leave conferred in the passport bearer's [entry clearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entry_Clearance). Under the [Schengen system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_Information_System), a foreign passport is stamped with a date stamp which does not indicate any duration of stay. This means the person is deemed to have permission to remain for either 3 months or the period shown on their visa, if specified otherwise. Member states of the [European Union](9317.md) are not permitted to place a stamp in the passport of a person who is not subject to immigration control. Stamping is prohibited because it imposes a control to which the person is not subject. Passport stamps may occasionally take the form of sticker stamps, such as entry stamps from [Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Japan). Depending on nationality, a visitor may not receive a stamp at all (unless specifically requested), such as an [EU](9317.md) or [EFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association) citizen travelling to an [EU or EFTA country](16565139.md), [Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Albania),[^cite_note-parlament.al-307] or [North Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_North_Macedonia).[^cite_note-mvr.gov.mk-308] Most countries issue exit stamps in addition to entry stamps. A few countries issue only entry stamps, including [Canada](23674739.md), [El Salvador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_El_Salvador), [Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Ireland), Mexico, [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_New_Zealand), [Singapore](27318.md), [United Kingdom](28258710.md) and the [United States of America](4766461.md). [Australia](4447229.md), [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Hong_Kong), [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Israel), [Macau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Macau) and [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_South_Korea) do not stamp passports upon entry nor exit. These countries or regions issue landing slips instead, except for Australia, which does not issue any physical evidence of entry. [Visas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_\(document\)) may also take the form of passport stamps.

Immigration authorities usually use different stamp styles for entries and exits to make it easier to track people's movements. Ink colour might be used to designate the mode of transportation (air, land, or sea), as in [Hong Kong](13404.md) before 1997, while border styles did the same in [Macau](19068.md). Other variations include changing the stamp size to indicate the length of stay, as in [Singapore](27318.md).

In many cases, passengers on [cruise ships](314855.md) do not receive passport stamps because the entire vessel has been cleared into port. It is often possible to get a souvenir stamp, although this requires finding the immigration office by the dock. In many cases, officials are used to such requests and will cooperate.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310] Also, as noted below, some of the smallest European countries will give a stamp on request, either at their border or tourist office charging, at most, a nominal fee.

### Exit controls

Indian entry stamp at New Delhi Indira Gandhi International Airport.

Indian exit stamp at Indira Gandhi International Airport.

Chinese entry and exit stamps at Shanghai Pudong International Airport.

India and China, like most countries, implement border controls at both entry and exit, and consequently stamp passports upon exit.



Entry stamp at [Lewiston–Queenston Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewiston%E2%80%93Queenston_Bridge), Ontario. Canada only conducts border control and stamps passports upon entry.

Whilst most countries implement border controls both at entry and exit, some jurisdictions do not. For instance, the United States and Canada do not implement exit controls at land borders and collect exit data on foreign nationals through airlines and by sharing information with neighbouring countries' entry border controls. These countries consequently do not issue exit stamps, even to travellers who require entry stamps. Similarly, Australia, Singapore, and South Korea have eliminated exit stamps even though they continue to implement brief border control checks upon exit for most foreign nationals. In countries where there is no formal control by immigration officials over travel documents at departure, exit information may be recorded by immigration authorities using information provided by transport operators.

*No exit control*:

- [United States](3434750.md) United States of America

  [^cite_note-311]

- [Canada](5042916.md) Canada

- [Mexico](3966054.md) Mexico (by air, but entrance declaration coupon is collected)

- [The Bahamas](3451.md) Bahamas

- [Republic of Ireland](14560.md) Ireland

- [United Kingdom](31717.md) United Kingdom

  [\[ak\]](#cite_note-313)

*Formal exit control without passport stamping*:

- [Albania](738.md) Albania (Entry & Exit stamp may be issued upon request)

- [Australia](4689264.md) Australia (Exit stamp issued upon explicit request)

  [^cite_note-Australia's_Arrival_and_Departure_procedures-314]

- [China](5405.md) China (Exit stamp issued upon request when using e-Gate)

- [Costa Rica](5551.md) Costa Rica (only at Costa Rican airports; different entry and exit stamps are made at the border crossing with [Panama](22997.md))

- [El Salvador](9356.md) El Salvador

- [Fiji](10707.md) Fiji

- [Hong Kong](13404.md) Hong Kong (no entry or exit stamps are issued; instead, landing slips are issued upon arrival only)

  [^cite_note-315]

- [Iran](14653.md) Iran

  [^cite_note-316]

- [Israel](9282173.md) Israel (no entry or exit stamps are issued at [Ben Gurion Airport](113149.md), instead landing slips are issued upon arrival and departure)

- [Japan](15573.md) Japan (Exit stamp issued upon request & when not using e-Gate since July 2019)

  [^cite_note-Japan_Begin_Screening_Departing_Foreign_Travelers_Facial_Recognition_Technology-317]

- [Macau](19068.md) Macau (no entry or exit stamps are issued; instead, landing slips are issued upon arrival only)

- [New Zealand](4913064.md) New Zealand

- [South Korea](27019.md) South Korea (since 1 November 2016)

- [Panama](22997.md) Panama (only at Panamanian airports; stamps are made at the border crossing with [Costa Rica](5551.md))

- [Taiwan](25734.md) Republic of China (exit stamp issued upon request & when not using e-Gate)

- [Singapore](27318.md) Singapore (no exit stamps since 22 April 2019)

  [^cite_note-318]

- [Saint Kitts and Nevis](27198.md) Saint Kitts and Nevis

- [Europe](9239.md) [Schengen Area](16565139.md) countries (when the [Entry/Exit System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entry/Exit_System) becomes operational, it is anticipated that the passports of third-country nationals will not be stamped when they enter and leave the Schengen Area)

### Exit permits

Some countries maintain controversial exit visa systems in addition to regular border controls. For instance, Uzbekistan requires its citizens to obtain exit visas before leaving for countries outside the [ CIS](36870.md), including Eastern European nations. Several [countries in the Arabian peninsula](220950.md) require exit visas for foreign workers under the [Kafala System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafala_System), meaning "sponsorship system"). Russia occasionally requires foreigners who overstay to obtain [exit visas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Russia#Exit) since one cannot exit Russia without a valid visa. Czechia has a similar policy.[^cite_note-319] Similarly, a foreign citizen granted a temporary residence permit in Russia needs an exit visa to take a trip abroad (valid for both exit and return). Not all foreign citizens are subject to that requirement. Citizens of Germany, for example, do not require this exit visa. During the [Cold War](325329.md), countries in the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) maintained strict controls on citizens' ability to travel abroad. Citizens of the Soviet Union, East Germany, and other communist states were typically required to obtain permission before travelling abroad. Unlike most of these states, citizens of [Yugoslavia](34244.md) enjoyed a significant freedom of international movement.[^cite_note-322]

Certain Asian countries have policies that similarly require specific categories of citizens to obtain official authorization before travelling or emigrating. This is usually either to enforce [national service](5735.md) obligations or to protect migrant workers from travelling to places where employers may abuse them. Singapore, for instance, operates an Exit Permit scheme to enforce the [national service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_service_in_Singapore) obligations of its male citizens and permanent residents.[^cite_note-323] These restrictions vary according to age and status.[^cite_note-324] [South Korea](27019.md) and [Taiwan](25734.md)[^cite_note-325] have similar policies. India, on the other hand, requires citizens who have not met certain educational requirements (and thus may be targeted by human traffickers or be coerced into [modern slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_slavery)) to apply for approval before leaving the country and endorses their passports with ["Emigration Check Required"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_Act,_1983). [Nepal](171166.md) similarly requires citizens emigrating to America on an [H-1B visa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H-1B_visa) to present an exit permit issued by the Ministry of Labour. This document, called a work permit, must be presented to immigration to leave the country.[^cite_note-326] In a bid to increase protection for the large amount of Indian, Bangladeshi, Chinese, and Nepali citizens smuggled through Indian airports to the [Middle East](19323.md) as underpaid labourers, many Indian airline companies require travellers to obtain an 'OK to Board' confirmation sent directly from visa authorities in certain GCC countries directly to the airline and will bar anyone who has not obtained this endorsement from clearing exit immigration.

[Eritrea](17238590.md) requires the vast majority of its citizens to obtain special authorization to leave the country or even to travel within it.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329]

### Travel documents

Main article: [Travel document](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_document)



Automated travel document inspection at [Dubai Airport](154742.md)

Border control policies typically require travellers to present valid travel documents to ascertain their identity, nationality, permanent residence status, and eligibility to enter a given jurisdiction. The most common form of travel document is the passport, a booklet-form identity document issued by national authorities or the governments of certain subnational territories[^cite_note-330] containing an individual's personal information as well as space for the authorities of other jurisdictions to affix stamps, visas, or other permits authorising the bearer to enter, reside, or travel within their territory. Certain jurisdictions permit individuals to clear border controls using identity cards, which typically contain similar personal information.

### Visas

Main article: [Travel visa](330265.md)





Tourist visas issued by India *(left)* and Singapore *(right)* in a stateless person's travel document



Brazilian multiple entry visa in an American passport



Tourist entry visa for mainland China



Sample of printed out eNTRI slip for Indian and mainland Chinese citizens to clear Malaysian border controls without a visa



Facilitated Rail Transit Document issued in Saint Petersburg for travel to Kaliningrad



Thai visa issued on arrival in stamp form

A visa is a travel document issued to foreign nationals enabling them to clear border controls. They traditionally take the form of an adhesive sticker or, occasionally, a stamp affixed to a page in an individual's passport or equivalent document. Visas policies different purposes depending on the priorities of each jurisdiction, ranging from ensuring that visitors do not pose a national security risk or have sufficient financial resources to simply functioning as a tax on tourists, as is the case with [countries like Mauritius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Mauritius) and other leisure destinations which issue visas on arrival, electronic visas, or electronic travel authorisations (ETAs) to most or all visitors. Visas may include limits on the duration of the foreigner's stay, areas within the state they may enter, the dates they may enter, the number of permitted visits, or an individual's right to work in the state in question.

Many countries in Asia have liberalised their visa controls in recent years to encourage transnational business and tourism. For example, [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_India), [Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Myanmar), and [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Sri_Lanka) have introduced electronic visas to ease border control for business travellers and tourists. [Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Malaysia) has introduced similar eVisa facilities and the eNTRI programme to expedite clearance for Indian and mainland Chinese citizens. [Thailand regularly issues](30334683.md) visas on arrival to many non-exempt visitors at major ports of entry to encourage tourism. Indonesia, in recent years, has progressively liberalized [its visa regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Indonesia), no longer requiring visas or on-arrival visas from most nationals, while [Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Singapore) has signed visa waiver agreements with many countries in recent years and has introduced electronic visa facilities for Indians, Eastern Europeans, and mainland Chinese. This trend towards visa liberalisation in Asia is part of the regional trend toward social and economic [globalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalisation) that has been linked to heightened economic growth.[^cite_note-331]

Certain countries, predominantly, though not exclusively, in Western Europe and the Americas, issue [working holiday visas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_holiday_visa) for younger visitors to supplement their travel funds by working in minor jobs. These are especially common among members of the European Union and elsewhere in Europe. Saudi Arabia issues a [special category visa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_Saudi_Arabia#Hajj_visas) for people on religious pilgrimage. Similar policies are in force in other countries with significant religious sites. Certain jurisdictions impose special visa requirements on [journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalism). Countries that require such visas include Cuba, [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_China), North Korea, Saudi Arabia, [America](4766461.md#I_visa), and Zimbabwe.

As a consequence of awkward border situations created by the fall of the Soviet Union, certain former members of the USSR and their neighbours maintain special visa-exemption policies for travellers transiting across international boundaries within a single country. For instance, Russia permits vehicles to transit through the [Saatse Boot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saatse_Boot) between the Estonian villages of [Lutepää](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lutep%C3%A4%C3%A4) and [Sesniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesniki) without a visa or border checkpoint, provided they do not stop. Similar provisions apply to the issuance of Facilitated Rail Transit Documents by Schengen Area members for travel between [Kaliningrad Oblast](17311.md) and the Russian mainland, enabling Russian citizens to travel to and from the exclave without a passport or visa.

Many countries allow individuals to clear border controls with foreign visas.[^cite_note-336] Notably, the Philippines permits nationals of India and China can use any of several foreign visas to clear border controls.[^cite_note-338] To encourage tourism by transit passengers, [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visa_policy_of_South_Korea) permits passengers in transit who would otherwise require a South Korean visa to enter for up to thirty days utilizing an Australian, Canadian, American, or Schengen visa. Uniquely, the British territory of Bermuda has ceased issuing its own visas and now requires travellers to either clear immigration visa-free in one of the three countries (Canada, America, and the United Kingdom) to/from which it has direct flights, or hold a visa for one of them.

### Electronic visas and electronic travel authorisations

Main article: [Electronic visa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_visa)

Beginning in the 2000s, many countries introduced e-visas and electronic travel authorisations (ETAs) as an alternative to traditional visas. An ETA is a form of pre-arrival registration, which may or may not be officially classified as a visa depending on the issuing jurisdiction, and is required for foreign travellers who are exempt from obtaining a full visa. In contrast to the procedures that typically apply regarding proper visas, under which the traveller normally has no recourse if rejected, if an ETA is rejected, the traveller can choose to apply for a visa instead. In contrast, an e-visa is a visa that travellers can apply for and obtain online, without visiting the issuing state's consular mission or visa agency. The following jurisdictions require certain categories of international travellers to hold an ETA or e-visa to clear border controls upon arrival:

- *Australia*:&#x20;

  - Electronic Travel Authority ([ETA](4447229.md#Electronic_visas))

### Nationality and travel history

Many nations implement border controls restricting the entry of people of certain nationalities or who have visited certain countries. For instance, Georgia refuses entry to holders of passports issued by the Republic of China.[^cite_note-timatic-365] Similarly, since April 2017, nationals of Bangladesh, Pakistan, Sudan, Syria, Yemen, and Iran have been banned from entering the parts of eastern Libya under the control of the [Tobruk government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_\(Libya\)).[^cite_note-timatic-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] The majority of [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) countries, as well as Iran and Malaysia, ban Israeli citizens,[^cite_note-timatic-365] however exceptional entry to Malaysia is possible with approval from the [Ministry of Home Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Home_Affairs_\(Malaysia\)).[^cite_note-368] Certain countries may also restrict entry to those with Israeli stamps or visas in their passports. As a result of tension over the [Artsakh](1000530.md) dispute, Azerbaijan currently forbids entry to Armenian citizens as well as to individuals with proof of travel to Artsakh.

Between September 2017 and January 2021, the United States did not issue new visas to nationals of Iran, North Korea, Libya, Somalia, Syria, or Yemen pursuant to [restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13780#Presidential_Proclamation_9645) imposed by the [Trump administration](52231341.md),[^cite_note-369] which were subsequently repealed by the [Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_administration) on 20 January 2021.[^cite_note-\:6-370] While in force, the restrictions were conditional and could be lifted if the countries affected meet the required security standards specified by the Trump administration, and [dual citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_citizenship) of these countries could still enter if they presented a passport from a non-designated country.

### Prescreening

A significant number of countries maintain prescreening facilities for passengers departing from other jurisdictions to clear border controls before arrival, thereby skipping [checkpoints](#Border_checkpoints) upon arrival. Aside from simplifying arrival formalities, this enables border control authorities to deny entry to potentially inadmissible travellers before they embark and to reduce congestion at border checkpoints at ports of arrival.

- **Hong Kong and mainland China**: There are two border crossings between Hong Kong and mainland China at which border controls imposed by the two jurisdictions are colocated:&#x20;

  - West Kowloon Railway Station ([simplified Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 香港西九龙站; [traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 香港西九龍站): A component of the [Guangzhou–Shenzhen–Hong Kong Express Rail Link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guangzhou%E2%80%93Shenzhen%E2%80%93Hong_Kong_Express_Rail_Link) ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 廣深港高速鐵路; [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin): *Guǎng–Shēn–Gǎng Gāosù Tiělù*), West Kowloon Station contains a "[Mainland Port Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong_West_Kowloon_railway_station#Mainland_Port_Area) ([simplified Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 站内地口岸区; [traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 站內地口岸區)", essentially enabling passengers and goods to clear mainland Chinese immigration on Hong Kong soil.

  - Shenzhen Bay Port ([simplified Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 深圳湾口岸; [traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 深圳灣口岸): The land border checkpoint at [Shenzhen Bay Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shenzhen_Bay_Port) in the mainland contains a Hong Kong Port Area ([simplified Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 港方口岸区; [traditional Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_Chinese_characters): 港方口岸區) which enables passengers and goods to clear Hong Kong border controls in the mainland. The checkpoint is located in mainland China on land leased from the city of [Shenzhen](83368.md) in [Guangdong](65109.md) province. By enabling travellers to clear both Chinese and Hong Kong border controls at a single location, it eliminates the need for a second checkpoint on the Hong Kong side of the [Shenzhen Bay Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong%E2%80%93Shenzhen_Western_Corridor).

Map of the upcoming Rapid Transit system

- **Singapore and Malaysia**:&#x20;

  - Woodlands Train Checkpoint ([Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language): *Pusat Pemeriksaan Kereta Api Woodlands*, [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 兀兰火车关卡, [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language): ஊட்லண்ட்ஸ் இரயில் மசாதலைச்சாவடிப): For cross-border rail passengers, Singaporean exit and Malaysian entry preclearance border controls are co-located at the [Woodlands Train Checkpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodlands_Train_Checkpoint) in Singapore, whilst Malaysian exit controls are located separately at [Johor Bahru Sentral railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Bahru_Sentral_railway_station) in Malaysia.

  - Johor Bahru – Singapore Rapid Transit System ([Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language): *Sistem Transit Aliran Johor Bahru–Singapura*, [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters): 新山－新加坡捷运系统, [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language): ஜோகூர் பாரு – சிங்கப்பூர் விரைவான போக்குவரத்து அமைப்பு, RTS): The upcoming [RTS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Bahru%E2%80%93Singapore_Rapid_Transit_System) connecting Singapore and Johor Bahru will feature border control preclearance both on the Singaporean side and on the Malaysian side. This will enable passengers arriving in Singapore from Malaysia or vice versa to proceed straight to their connecting transport, since the RTS will link to both the [Singapore MRT system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_Rapid_Transit_\(Singapore\)) ([Thomson–East Coast Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomson%E2%80%93East_Coast_Line)) and [Johor Bahru Sentral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Bahru_Sentral_railway_station). Unlike the preclearance systems adopted in America and Hong Kong, but similar to the United Kingdom's juxtaposed controls, this system will mitigate the need for arrival border controls on both sides of the border.

    [^cite_note-iskandar-371]

    [^cite_note-372]

- **Malaysia and Thailand**:&#x20;

  - Padang Besar railway station ([Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language): สถานีรถไฟปาดังเบซาร์, [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language): *Stesen keretapi Padang Besar*): The [Padang Besar railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padang_Besar_railway_station) in [Padang Besar, Malaysia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padang_Besar,_Malaysia) has co-located border control facilities for both Malaysia and Thailand. However, the station is entirely within Malaysian territory (albeit just 200 metres south of the [Malaysia-Thailand border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia-Thailand_border)). The facilities for each country operate from separate counters inside the railway station building at the platform level.

    [^cite_note-373]

    &#x20;Passengers entering Thailand clear Malaysian and Thai border formalities here in Malaysian territory before boarding their [State Railway of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Railway_of_Thailand) trains which then cross the actual borderline several minutes after departing the station. Passengers from Thailand entering Malaysia are also processed here using the same counters, as there are no separate counters for entry and exit in either country.

- **United Kingdom and the Schengen Area**: Border control for travel between the United Kingdom and the Schengen Area features significant prescreening under the [juxtaposed controls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juxtaposed_controls) programme for travel both by ferry and rail. This includes customs and immigration prescreening on both sides of the [Channel Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Tunnel),

  [\[au\]](#cite_note-375)

  &#x20;and immigration-only prescreening for ferry passengers and on the [Eurostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostar) between the United Kingdom and stations located in Belgium, France, and the Netherlands. Eurostar and Eurotunnel passengers departing from the Schengen area go through both French, Dutch, or Belgian exit border control and British entry border controls before departures, while passengers departing from the United Kingdom, including those departing for Belgium or the Netherlands, undergo French border controls on British soil. For travel by ferry, French entry border control for ferries between Dover and Calais or [Dunkerque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunkirk) takes place at the [Port of Dover](6142372.md), whilst French exit and British entry border control take place at [Calais](85468.md) and Dunkerque. For rail travel, 12 border control checkpoints are currently in operation.

  [\[av\]](#cite_note-379)
